55	
56	\subsection{Effective Discriminable Depth}
57	\label{sec:atlas:edd}
58	
59	Aggregate accuracy masks that adjacent depths become harder to distinguish as depth increases; we quantify this via the \emph{Effective Discriminable Depth} (EDD).
60	Formally, given binary pairwise AUC scores for consecutive depth pairs:
61	\begin{equation}
62	\mathrm{EDD}_T \;=\; \max\bigl\{d^* : \mathrm{AUC}(d^*,\, d^*{+}1) \geq T\bigr\}
63	\label{eq:edd}
64	\end{equation}
65	where $\mathrm{AUC}(d, d{+}1)$ is the area under the ROC curve of a dedicated binary classifier distinguishing depth~$d$ from depth~$d{+}1$, and $T$ is the minimum AUC at which an adjacent pair is considered discriminable.
66	We adopt $T{=}0.75$ as default---the midpoint between chance (0.5) and perfect discrimination (1.0)---and report sensitivity across $T \in \{0.65, 0.70, 0.75, 0.80\}$ in Table~\ref{tab:edd_sensitivity}.
67	Crucially, $\mathrm{AUC}(d, d{+}1)$ must be estimated from a dedicated binary classifier rather than derived from a multi-class pipeline, as the latter suffers from decision boundary dilution (\S\ref{sec:atlas:edd:dilution}).
68	
69	For each adjacent pair we train a dedicated binary RF (500 trees, 5-fold stratified CV) and compute $\mathrm{AUC}(d, d{+}1)$ per Eq.~\ref{eq:edd}.
70	
71	Within the 4-class atlas (d0--d3), 7 of 9 cells achieve $\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}=3$; the two exceptions are Pythia and OLMo with top-$k$ sampling ($\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}=2$), consistent with top-$k$'s signal-attenuating effect noted in \S\ref{sec:atlas:indist}.
72	
73	We extend the Pythia-1.4B nucleus pipeline to 6 depths (d0--d5) and train a 500-tree binary RF for each adjacent pair.
74	Figure~\ref{fig:auc_decay} shows the pairwise AUC decay curve.
```

```text
85	The statistical strength of the d3\,vs.\,d4 boundary varies across models despite directional agreement.
86	Bootstrap permutation tests comparing $\mathrm{AUC}(d3,d4)$ and $\mathrm{AUC}(d4,d5)$ yield $p{=}0.001$ for Pythia (the d3/d4 margin is reliably above d4/d5), $p{=}0.091$ for OLMo (marginal), and $p{=}0.505$ for GPT-2~XL (not statistically significant).
87	This stratification reflects the tightness of the AUC gap: all three models place d3\,vs.\,d4 above and d4\,vs.\,d5 below the $T{=}0.75$ line, but only Pythia's margin is large enough for conventional significance.
88	We therefore interpret EDD as a continuous decay profile rather than a single integer, noting that $\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}$ coincides at~4 across all three models while the underlying confidence varies substantially.
89	
90	\begin{table}[t]
91	\centering
92	\small
93	\caption{Binary pairwise AUC for adjacent depths across three models (nucleus $p{=}0.95$, d0--d5). d0\,vs.\,d1 omitted (AUC$\geq$0.99 for all). $p$: bootstrap permutation test of $\mathrm{AUC}(d3,d4) > \mathrm{AUC}(d4,d5)$. Experiment IDs: \texttt{depth\_extension\_binary\_pairwise\_002}, \texttt{depth\_extension\_gpt2xl\_d4d5\_001}, \texttt{depth\_extension\_olmo\_d4d5\_002}.}
94	\label{tab:cross_model_auc}
95	\begin{tabular}{lccccl}
96	\toprule
97	Model & d1/d2 & d2/d3 & d3/d4 & d4/d5 & $p$ \\
98	\midrule
99	Pythia-1.4B & .925 & .823 & .784 & .683 & .001 \\
100	GPT-2 XL & .966 & .919 & .750 & .681 & .505 \\
101	OLMo-1B & .968 & .843 & .765 & .684 & .091 \\
102	\bottomrule
103	\end{tabular}
104	\end{table}
105	
106	\begin{figure}[t]
107	\centering
108	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{../../figures/paper/fig_auc_decay.pdf}
109	\caption{Binary pairwise AUC between adjacent depths (Pythia-1.4B, nucleus $p{=}0.95$, d0--d5). Shaded bands: 95\% bootstrap CI. Dashed line: $T{=}0.75$. $\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}=4$: depth pairs up to d3\,vs.\,d4 remain discriminable.}

```text
110	\label{fig:auc_decay}
111	\end{figure}
112	
113	Table~\ref{tab:edd_sensitivity} shows $\mathrm{EDD}_T$ across all three models and four threshold choices.
114	All three models yield identical EDD at every $T$, reflecting the tight clustering of d3\,vs.\,d4 AUC near 0.75 and d4\,vs.\,d5 AUC near 0.68 (Table~\ref{tab:cross_model_auc}).
115	At $T{=}0.65$, all pairs through d4\,vs.\,d5 pass, giving $\mathrm{EDD}=5$; at $T{=}0.80$, the d3\,vs.\,d4 boundary falls below threshold for all models, yielding $\mathrm{EDD}=3$.
116	
117	\begin{table}[t]
118	\centering
119	\small
120	\caption{$\mathrm{EDD}_T$ across three models (nucleus $p{=}0.95$) and four thresholds. All three models yield identical EDD at every $T$ despite differing pairwise AUC values (Table~\ref{tab:cross_model_auc}). Experiment ID: \texttt{granularity\_ablation\_rf\_001}.}
121	\label{tab:edd_sensitivity}
122	\begin{tabular}{cccc}
123	\toprule
124	$T$ & Pythia-1.4B & GPT-2 XL & OLMo-1B \\
125	\midrule
126	0.65 & 5 & 5 & 5 \\
127	0.70 & 4 & 4 & 4 \\
128	0.75 & 4 & 4 & 4 \\
129	0.80 & 3 & 3 & 3 \\
130	\bottomrule
131	\end{tabular}
132	\end{table}
133	
134	\paragraph{Decision boundary dilution.}
135	\label{sec:atlas:edd:dilution}
136	EDD's binary-pair design is motivated by \emph{decision boundary dilution}: when a multi-class classifier must simultaneously separate $K$ depths, its decision boundaries are shared across all $\binom{K}{2}$ class pairs, diluting the capacity allocated to any single adjacent pair.
137	Concretely, in the Pythia nucleus setting (experiment \texttt{granularity\_ablation\_rf\_001}), the d2\,vs.\,d3 pairwise AUC degrades from 0.804 in a 4-class pipeline to 0.689 in a 6-class pipeline---a 14.3\% relative decline---despite identical underlying data.
138	Mean classification accuracy likewise drops from 99.5\% (binary) to 78.3\% (4-class) to 62.9\% (6-class).
139	Because the pairwise signal between depths $d$ and $d{+}1$ does not change when unrelated depths are added, this degradation is entirely attributable to boundary dilution, not signal loss.
```

{
  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513_2/projects/recursive_gen_depth_est/docs/paper/atlas.tex",
  "old_string": "Aggregate accuracy masks that adjacent depths become harder to distinguish as depth increases; we quantify this via the \\emph{Effective Discriminable Depth} (EDD).\nFormally, given binary pairwise AUC scores for consecutive depth pairs:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\mathrm{EDD}_T \\;=\\; \\max\\bigl\\{d^* : \\mathrm{AUC}(d^*,\\, d^*{+}1) \\geq T\\bigr\\}\n\\label{eq:edd}\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\mathrm{AUC}(d, d{+}1)$ is the area under the ROC curve of a dedicated binary classifier distinguishing depth~$d$ from depth~$d{+}1$, and $T$ is the minimum AUC at which an adjacent pair is considered discriminable.\nWe adopt $T{=}0.75$ as default---the midpoint between chance (0.5) and perfect discrimination (1.0)---and report sensitivity across $T \\in \\{0.65, 0.70, 0.75, 0.80\\}$ in Table~\\ref{tab:edd_sensitivity}.\nCrucially, $\\mathrm{AUC}(d, d{+}1)$ must be estimated from a dedicated binary classifier rather than derived from a multi-class pipeline, as the latter suffers from decision boundary dilution (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas:edd:dilution}).\n\nFor each adjacent pair we train a dedicated binary RF (500 trees, 5-fold stratified CV) and compute $\\mathrm{AUC}(d, d{+}1)$ per Eq.~\\ref{eq:edd}.",
  "new_string": "Aggregate accuracy masks that adjacent depths become harder to distinguish as depth increases; we quantify this via the \\emph{Effective Discriminable Depth} (EDD).\nWe adopt the formal definition from Eq.~\\ref{eq:edd} with default threshold $T{=}0.75$---the midpoint between chance (0.5) and perfect discrimination (1.0)---and report sensitivity across $T \\in \\{0.65, 0.70, 0.75, 0.80\\}$ in Table~\\ref{tab:edd_sensitivity}.\nCrucially, each pairwise AUC must be estimated from a dedicated binary classifier rather than derived from a multi-class pipeline, as the latter suffers from decision boundary dilution (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas:edd:dilution}).\n\nFor each adjacent pair we train a dedicated binary RF (500 trees, 5-fold stratified CV)."
}
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01R6UD7YLQ6UpsdYnQj8ys6v`</summary>

```text
95	
96	\paragraph{Temperature-induced boundary inversion.}
97	Training on nucleus + top-$k$ and testing on temperature yields d1v2 AUC of 0.129 for GPT-2~XL and 0.253 for OLMo---both below 0.5, indicating that the d1/d2 decision boundary \emph{inverts} (Pythia retains a degraded 0.576), consistent with the accelerated convergence in \S\ref{sec:atlas:trajectories}.
98	Z-score normalization \cite{sugiyama2007_importance_weighting} improves logistic regression cross-strategy accuracy (25\%$\to$61\%) but does not recover the inversion---all three models worsen (GPT-2~XL 0.50$\to$0.24, OLMo 0.50$\to$0.19, Pythia 0.63$\to$0.52)---confirming a genuine geometry-level shift rather than a correctable scale difference.
99	
100	\subsection{End-to-End Learning}
101	\label{sec:gen:dcol}
102	
103	We finetune DeBERTa-v3-base and RoBERTa-base end-to-end (lr$\,{=}\,2{\times}10^{-5}$, 3 epochs, 5-fold CV) on Pythia-1.4B nucleus data.
104	Both converge to ${\sim}$59\% (RoBERTa 59.5$\pm$3.2\%, DeBERTa 58.3$\pm$4.7\%), 19pp below feature-based RF (78.2\%); the 1.2pp inter-backbone gap rules out architecture selection bias.
105	Both easily separate d0 (F1$>$0.95) but fail at adjacent-depth discrimination (d1/d2 F1$<$0.42).
106	Probing reveals a negative correlation between PLM attention and RF permutation importance for both backbones (DeBERTa $\rho{=}{-}0.484$, RoBERTa $\rho{=}{-}0.456$; zero top-5 overlap; Appendix~\ref{app:feature_overlap}), confirming that PLMs encode surface lexical cues while our features capture perplexity tail structure inaccessible to end-to-end learning.
107	
108	DCOL \cite{cloc2025}, which adds a contrastive ordinal loss, fared worse: across six iterations with two backbones (Appendix~\ref{app:dcol_iterations}), the best run (RoBERTa-large) reached only 64.5\% with severe overfitting, 13.7pp below RF.
109	That all end-to-end approaches---standard finetuning (58--60\%), DCOL (64.5\%)---substantially underperform feature-based methods (78.2\%) confirms that depth estimation requires explicit distributional statistics rather than learned text representations.
```

</details>

      "content": "\n\\paragraph{Temperature-induced boundary inversion.}\nTraining on nucleus + top-$k$ and testing on temperature yields d1v2 AUC of 0.129 for GPT-2~XL and 0.253 for OLMo---both below 0.5, indicating that the d1/d2 decision boundary \\emph{inverts} (Pythia retains a degraded 0.576), consistent with the accelerated convergence in \\S\\ref{sec:atlas:trajectories}.\nZ-score normalization \\cite{sugiyama2007_importance_weighting} improves logistic regression cross-strategy accuracy (25\\%$\\to$61\\%) but does not recover the inversion---all three models worsen (GPT-2~XL 0.50$\\to$0.24, OLMo 0.50$\\to$0.19, Pythia 0.63$\\to$0.52)---confirming a genuine geometry-level shift rather than a correctable scale difference.\n\n\\subsection{End-to-End Learning}\n\\label{sec:gen:dcol}\n\nWe finetune DeBERTa-v3-base and RoBERTa-base end-to-end (lr$\\,{=}\\,2{\\times}10^{-5}$, 3 epochs, 5-fold CV) on Pythia-1.4B nucleus data.\nBoth converge to ${\\sim}$59\\% (RoBERTa 59.5$\\pm$3.2\\%, DeBERTa 58.3$\\pm$4.7\\%), 19pp below feature-based RF (78.2\\%); the 1.2pp inter-backbone gap rules out architecture selection bias.\nBoth easily separate d0 (F1$>$0.95) but fail at adjacent-depth discrimination (d1/d2 F1$<$0.42).\nProbing reveals a negative correlation between PLM attention and RF permutation importance for both backbones (DeBERTa $\\rho{=}{-}0.484$, RoBERTa $\\rho{=}{-}0.456$; zero top-5 overlap; Appendix~\\ref{app:feature_overlap}), confirming that PLMs encode surface lexical cues while our features capture perplexity tail structure inaccessible to end-to-end learning.\n\nDCOL \\cite{cloc2025}, which adds a contrastive ordinal loss, fared worse: across six iterations with two backbones (Appendix~\\ref{app:dcol_iterations}), the best run (RoBERTa-large) reached only 64.5\\% with severe overfitting, 13.7pp below RF.\nThat all end-to-end approaches---standard finetuning (58--60\\%), DCOL (64.5\\%)---substantially underperform feature-based methods (78.2\\%) confirms that depth estimation requires explicit distributional statistics rather than learned text representations.",
      "numLines": 15,
      "startLine": 95,
      "totalLines": 109
    }
  },

### Tool result

_source line 2963 · 2026-05-15T16:02:18.165Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01RM6Sx2FfNTGgNPorrbrikD`</summary>

```text
103	We finetune DeBERTa-v3-base and RoBERTa-base end-to-end (lr$\,{=}\,2{\times}10^{-5}$, 3 epochs, 5-fold CV) on Pythia-1.4B nucleus data.
104	Both converge to ${\sim}$59\% (RoBERTa 59.5$\pm$3.2\%, DeBERTa 58.3$\pm$4.7\%), 19pp below feature-based RF (78.2\%); the 1.2pp inter-backbone gap rules out architecture selection bias.
105	Both easily separate d0 (F1$>$0.95) but fail at adjacent-depth discrimination (d1/d2 F1$<$0.42).
106	Probing reveals a negative correlation between PLM attention and RF permutation importance for both backbones (DeBERTa $\rho{=}{-}0.484$, RoBERTa $\rho{=}{-}0.456$; zero top-5 overlap; Appendix~\ref{app:feature_overlap}), confirming that PLMs encode surface lexical cues while our features capture perplexity tail structure inaccessible to end-to-end learning.
107	
108	DCOL \cite{cloc2025}, which adds a contrastive ordinal loss, fared worse: across six iterations with two backbones (Appendix~\ref{app:dcol_iterations}), the best run (RoBERTa-large) reached only 64.5\% with severe overfitting, 13.7pp below RF.
109	That all end-to-end approaches---standard finetuning (58--60\%), DCOL (64.5\%)---substantially underperform feature-based methods (78.2\%) confirms that depth estimation requires explicit distributional statistics rather than learned text representations.
```

</details>

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01URordZBVozBnHXnWs6ww8t`</summary>

```text
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513_2/projects/recursive_gen_depth_est/docs/paper/atlas.tex:39:The 90th-percentile perplexity (\texttt{p90\_ppl}) ranks among the top-2 most important features in all 9 cells (100\%); surprisal variance (\texttt{var\_surprisal}) reaches top-2 in 7 of 9 cells (78\%).
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513_2/projects/recursive_gen_depth_est/docs/paper/atlas.tex:46:Token-level perplexity distributions are heavy-tailed: individual outlier passages can dominate the arithmetic mean of their depth class, whereas \texttt{p90\_ppl} is robust to such extremes while still capturing the tail erosion that signals recursive depth.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513_2/projects/recursive_gen_depth_est/docs/paper/atlas.tex:49:At shallow boundaries (d1 vs.\ d2), \texttt{var\_surprisal} dominates importance; at deeper boundaries (d3 vs.\ d4, d4 vs.\ d5), tail perplexity statistics (\texttt{p90\_ppl}, \texttt{kurtosis\_ppl}) take over.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513_2/projects/recursive_gen_depth_est/docs/paper/atlas.tex:55:\caption{Top-5 features by RF importance in each atlas cell. \textbf{Bold}: features appearing in the top-2. \texttt{p90\_ppl} reaches top-2 in all 9 cells; \texttt{var\_surprisal} in 7/9.}
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513_2/projects/recursive_gen_depth_est/docs/paper/atlas.tex:81:Perplexity tail statistics (\texttt{p90\_ppl}, \texttt{mean\_surprisal}) decrease monotonically with depth ($\rho = -1.0$ across cell-level medians), reflecting the narrowing of the token-predictability distribution under recursive finetuning.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513_2/projects/recursive_gen_depth_est/docs/paper/atlas.tex:82:Repetition features (\texttt{rep\_2gram}, \texttt{rep\_3gram}, \texttt{rep\_4gram}) increase monotonically, capturing the growing self-repetition that accompanies distribution collapse.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513_2/projects/recursive_gen_depth_est/docs/paper/atlas.tex:92:\caption{Feature trajectories across depths $d{=}0$ to $d{=}3$, stratified by model (columns) and strategy (line style). Top row: \texttt{p90\_ppl} (monotonically decreasing). Middle: \texttt{var\_surprisal} (decreasing). Bottom: \texttt{rep\_3gram} (increasing). Shaded bands: interquartile range. The $d{=}0 \to d{=}1$ gap exceeds all subsequent inter-depth gaps.}
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513_2/projects/recursive_gen_depth_est/docs/paper/atlas.tex:147:Concretely, in the Pythia nucleus setting (experiment \texttt{granularity\_ablation\_rf\_001}), the d2\,vs.\,d3 pairwise AUC degrades from 0.804 in a 4-class pipeline to 0.689 in a 6-class pipeline---a 14.3\% relative decline---despite identical underlying data.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513_2/projects/recursive_gen_depth_est/docs/paper/atlas.tex:167:For GPT-2~XL, temperature depth-1 \texttt{p90\_ppl} median is 92.2, falling within the nucleus/top-$k$ depth-2 range of 65--92.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513_2/projects/recursive_gen_depth_est/docs/paper/method.tex:34:LoRA targets are architecture-specific: \texttt{query\_key\_value} for Pythia (GPT-NeoX), \texttt{q\_proj}/\texttt{k\_proj}/\texttt{v\_proj} for OLMo, and \texttt{c\_attn}/\texttt{c\_proj} for GPT-2~XL.
```

_source line 2994 · 2026-05-15T16:30:02.187Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01U16no9y3G1KoVy3SWgQhN5`</summary>

```text
86	
87	For each adjacent depth pair $(d, d{+}1)$, we train a dedicated binary Random Forest (500 trees; increased from 200 in the multi-class setting to ensure convergence on the smaller per-pair samples) and compute the one-vs-one AUC.
88	Given a threshold $T$, we define:
89	\begin{equation}
90	\mathrm{EDD}_T = \max \{ d^*{+}1 : \mathrm{AUC}(d^*, d^*{+}1) \geq T \}
91	\label{eq:edd}
92	\end{equation}
93	where $d^* \in \{0, 1, \dots, K{-}1\}$.
94	The default threshold is $T{=}0.75$; we report sensitivity analyses at $T \in \{0.65, 0.70, 0.75, 0.80\}$ in \S\ref{sec:atlas}.
95	Crucially, each AUC is obtained from an independent binary pairwise classifier (5-fold stratified CV) rather than extracted from a single multi-class model, avoiding decision boundary dilution (\S\ref{sec:atlas}).
96	
97	EDD captures a natural question: \emph{how deep into the recursive chain can we reliably distinguish successive generations?}
98	A high EDD indicates that the statistical fingerprints retain discriminative power across many recursion steps; a low EDD signals rapid convergence to an indistinguishable regime.
99	
```

</details>

98	\label{sec:atlas:edd}
99	
100	We extend the benchmark to depth $d{=}5$ for all three models under nucleus sampling.
101	Each model's depth-3 LoRA checkpoint is used to finetune and generate depth-4 data, and the process repeats for depth~5; feature extraction uses the same reference model (GPT-2 base, 124M).
102	We train dedicated binary RF classifiers (500 trees) for each adjacent pair, as defined in Eq.~\ref{eq:edd}.
103	
104	\paragraph{AUC decay profile.}
105	Figure~\ref{fig:auc_decay} plots the binary pairwise AUC decay curves.
```

_source line 3017 · 2026-05-15T16:30:36.822Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01Te52536uaDQXwXrYAJtAzx`</summary>

```text
105	Figure~\ref{fig:auc_decay} plots the binary pairwise AUC decay curves.
106	For Pythia-1.4B (nucleus), AUC decays monotonically: d0-vs-d1 $= 0.999$, d1-vs-d2 $= 0.925$, d2-vs-d3 $= 0.823$, d3-vs-d4 $= 0.784$, d4-vs-d5 $= 0.683$.
107	The steepest drop occurs between the d1-vs-d2 and d2-vs-d3 pairs; the curve then flattens as the system approaches an indistinguishable regime gradually rather than crossing a sharp boundary.
108	
109	\paragraph{Cross-model convergence at depth 4--5.}
110	All three models exhibit a consistent profile at the deep boundaries.
111	The d3-vs-d4 AUC clusters near the $T{=}0.75$ threshold: Pythia $0.784$ (bootstrap 95\% CI $[0.765, 0.802]$, $p = 0.001$), OLMo $0.765$ ($[0.744, 0.785]$, $p = 0.091$), GPT-2~XL $0.750$ ($[0.728, 0.771]$, $p = 0.505$), where $p$ denotes the one-tailed bootstrap probability under $H_0\!: \mathrm{AUC} \leq 0.75$ (1{,}000 iterations).
112	Only Pythia achieves significance at conventional levels; OLMo is marginal and GPT-2~XL falls on the threshold despite its higher in-distribution accuracy.
113	The d4-vs-d5 AUC falls uniformly below 0.70 across all three models (Pythia $0.683$, OLMo $0.684$, GPT-2~XL $0.681$), confirming a model-independent signal saturation point at depth~5.
114	
115	This pattern---directional consistency across models with graded statistical strength---supports interpreting the d3--d4 region as a \emph{transition zone} rather than a binary boundary.
116	The continuous AUC decay curve provides a more informative characterization of depth discriminability than any single $\mathrm{EDD}_T$ value.
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_014QPxY5FZFdsrk93kkPTEdH`</summary>

```text
140	\caption{Binary pairwise AUC decay curves for three models (nucleus sampling, depths 0--5). Horizontal dashed lines mark $T \in \{0.65, 0.70, 0.75, 0.80\}$. All three curves converge below 0.70 at d4-vs-d5, while d3-vs-d4 AUC clusters in the range 0.750--0.784. Shaded bands: bootstrap 95\% CIs.}
141	\label{fig:auc_decay}
142	\end{figure}
143	
144	\paragraph{Decision boundary dilution.}
145	\label{sec:atlas:edd:dilution}
146	EDD's binary-pair design is motivated by \emph{decision boundary dilution}: when a multi-class classifier must simultaneously separate $K$ depths, its decision boundaries are shared across all $\binom{K}{2}$ class pairs, diluting the capacity allocated to any single adjacent pair.
147	Concretely, in the Pythia nucleus setting (experiment \texttt{granularity\_ablation\_rf\_001}), the d2\,vs.\,d3 pairwise AUC degrades from 0.804 in a 4-class pipeline to 0.689 in a 6-class pipeline---a 14.3\% relative decline---despite identical underlying data.
148	Mean classification accuracy likewise drops from 99.5\% (binary) to 78.3\% (4-class) to 62.9\% (6-class).
149	Because the pairwise signal between depths $d$ and $d{+}1$ does not change when unrelated depths are added, this degradation is entirely attributable to boundary dilution, not signal loss.
150	Binary pairwise classifiers eliminate this confound, isolating the true adjacent-pair discriminability that EDD is designed to measure.
151	
152	%% ----------------------------------------------------------------
153	\subsection{Strategy-Dependent Trajectories}
154	\label{sec:atlas:strategy}
```

{

  "parentUuid": "130fdb40-b5e8-4f7c-9a81-99f4ed0bbb96",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "promptId": "9c1b5bbf-d9e3-4289-9930-fb30eec16ff2",
  "type": "user",
  "uuid": "5494c69b-0058-4d19-8916-490fbf30fcdd",
  "toolUseResult": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513_2/projects/recursive_gen_depth_est/docs/paper/method.tex",
    "oldString": "We adopt standard LoRA hyperparameters ($r{=}16$, $\\alpha{=}32$, dropout$\\,{=}\\,0.05$) following \\citet{hu2022lora}, applied uniformly across all models.",
    "newString": "We adopt commonly used LoRA hyperparameters ($r{=}16$, $\\alpha{=}32$, dropout$\\,{=}\\,0.05$) following \\citet{hu2022lora}, applied uniformly across all models.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Task Formulation}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\n\\subsection{Problem Definition}\n\\label{sec:method:problem}\n\nLet $f_\\theta$ denote a language model and $\\mathcal{D}_0$ a corpus of human-written seed text.\nWe define a \\emph{recursive generation chain} of depth $K$ as the sequence $\\mathcal{D}_0, \\mathcal{D}_1, \\dots, \\mathcal{D}_K$, where each $\\mathcal{D}_k$ ($k \\geq 1$) is produced by finetuning $f_\\theta$ on $\\mathcal{D}_{k-1}$ and sampling from the resulting model.\nDepth $d{=}0$ denotes human-written text; depth $d{=}k$ ($k \\geq 1$) denotes text generated by a model trained on depth-$(k{-}1)$ data.\n\nGiven a text passage $x$, the task is to estimate its recursive generation depth $d \\in \\{0, 1, \\dots, K\\}$.\nThis is an ordinal classification problem: depths carry a natural ordering, and $d{=}1$ is structurally closer to $d{=}0$ than $d{=}3$ is, since each generation step compounds distributional drift.\n\n\\subsection{Multi-Generational Benchmark Construction}\n\\label{sec:method:benchmark}\n\nWe construct a benchmark spanning three axes: model family, decoding strategy, and generation depth.\n\n\\paragraph{Model families.}\nWe select three 1--1.5B-parameter models with diverse pretraining corpora and architectures: Pythia-1.4B (EleutherAI), OLMo-1B (AI2), and GPT-2~XL (OpenAI, 1.5B).\nRestricting to a narrow parameter range isolates the effect of architecture and training data from scale.\n\n\\paragraph{Decoding strategies.}\nEach model is paired with three decoding strategies: nucleus sampling ($p{=}0.95$) \\cite{holtzman2020_nucleus}, temperature sampling ($\\tau{=}0.9$), and top-$k$ sampling ($k{=}50$).\nThese span the dominant modes of open-ended generation and induce qualitatively different entropy profiles.\n\n\\paragraph{Depth and scale.}\nThe primary benchmark covers depths d0--d3 across all $3 \\times 3 = 9$ model--strategy cells, with 5{,}000 samples per depth per cell (180K samples total).\nFor depth extension experiments (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas}), we extend one pipeline (Pythia-1.4B, nucleus) to d0--d5.\n\n\\paragraph{Generation pipeline.}\nStarting from a Wikipedia subset as seed text ($\\mathcal{D}_0$), each generation cycle proceeds in three steps: (1)~LoRA finetuning \\cite{hu2022lora} with rank $r{=}16$, $\\alpha{=}32$, learning rate $2{\\times}10^{-4}$, batch size 8, for 3 epochs; (2)~generation of 5{,}000 passages (max 512 tokens) under the specified decoding strategy; (3)~the generated corpus becomes $\\mathcal{D}_k$ and serves as training data for the next cycle.\nEach generation cycle samples unconditionally from the BOS token; there is no per-passage lineage across depths, ensuring that cross-validation splits do not introduce information leakage.\nLoRA targets are architecture-specific: \\texttt{query\\_key\\_value} for Pythia (GPT-NeoX), \\texttt{q\\_proj}/\\texttt{k\\_proj}/\\texttt{v\\_proj} for OLMo, and \\texttt{c\\_attn}/\\texttt{c\\_proj} for GPT-2~XL.\nWe adopt standard LoRA hyperparameters ($r{=}16$, $\\alpha{=}32$, dropout$\\,{=}\\,0.05$) following \\citet{hu2022lora}, applied uniformly across all models.\n\n\\paragraph{Reference model.}\nAll statistical features (\\S\\ref{sec:method:features}) are computed using a fixed reference model---GPT-2 base (124M)---that is independent of the three generation models.\nThe reference model computes per-token perplexity for each passage, providing a consistent measurement frame across model families.\nIn cross-model leave-one-out evaluation (\\S\\ref{sec:generalization}), the reference model remains fixed even when GPT-2~XL is held out; the partial architectural overlap this introduces is discussed in Limitations.\n\n\\subsection{Statistical Feature Design}\n\\label{sec:method:features}\n\nWe extract a 19-dimensional feature vector $\\mathbf{f}(x)$ from the reference-model perplexity distribution over each passage $x$.\nThe features fall into four categories (Table~\\ref{tab:features}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{The 19 statistical features comprising $\\mathbf{f}(x)$, grouped by category. All perplexity and surprisal features are computed from the reference model (GPT-2 base, 124M).}\n\\label{tab:features}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llr@{}}\n\\toprule\nCategory & Features & $n$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nPerplexity & mean, var, skewness, kurtosis, & 9 \\\\\nstatistics & p10, p25, p50, p75, p90 & \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nSurprisal & mean, var, entropy & 3 \\\\\nstatistics & & \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nLexical & type--token ratio, hapax ratio, & 4 \\\\\ndiversity & bigram entropy, trigram entropy & \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nRepetition & repeated 2/3/4-gram fractions & 3 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\emph{Perplexity statistics} (9 features) capture the location, spread, and shape of the per-token perplexity distribution via its mean, variance, skewness, kurtosis, and five percentile quantiles (p10--p90).\nThe tail quantiles (p75, p90) prove especially informative: recursive finetuning erodes the high-perplexity tail, making these features monotonically decreasing with depth (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas}).\n\n\\emph{Surprisal statistics} (3 features) operate on $-\\log p(\\text{token})$ and include mean surprisal, surprisal variance, and the entropy of the surprisal distribution.\nWhile correlated with perplexity features, surprisal variance captures second-order structure---the variability of token predictability---that is not redundant with perplexity moments.\n\n\\emph{Lexical diversity} (4 features) measures vocabulary usage through type--token ratio, hapax ratio (fraction of tokens appearing exactly once), and bigram/trigram entropy.\n\n\\emph{Repetition} (3 features) quantifies the fraction of repeated $n$-grams for $n \\in \\{2, 3, 4\\}$, capturing the increased self-repetition characteristic of recursive generation.\n\n\\subsection{Effective Discriminable Depth}\n\\label{sec:method:edd}\n\nAggregate classification accuracy over $K{+}1$ classes obscures whether discriminability degrades at specific depths.\nWe introduce the \\emph{Effective Discriminable Depth} (EDD), a scalar metric that summarizes how far into a recursive chain adjacent depths remain distinguishable.\n\nFor each adjacent depth pair $(d, d{+}1)$, we train a dedicated binary Random Forest (500 trees; increased from 200 in the multi-class setting to ensure convergence on the smaller per-pair samples) and compute the one-vs-one AUC.\nGiven a threshold $T$, we define:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\mathrm{EDD}_T = \\max \\{ d^*{+}1 : \\mathrm{AUC}(d^*, d^*{+}1) \\geq T \\}\n\\label{eq:edd}\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $d^* \\in \\{0, 1, \\dots, K{-}1\\}$.\nThe default threshold is $T{=}0.75$; we report sensitivity analyses at $T \\in \\{0.65, 0.70, 0.75, 0.80\\}$ in \\S\\ref{sec:atlas}.\nCrucially, each AUC is obtained from an independent binary pairwise classifier (5-fold stratified CV) rather than extracted from a single multi-class model, avoiding decision boundary dilution (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas}).\n\nEDD captures a natural question: \\emph{how deep into the recursive chain can we reliably distinguish successive generations?}\nA high EDD indicates that the statistical fingerprints retain discriminative power across many recursion steps; a low EDD signals rapid convergence to an indistinguishable regime.\n\n\\paragraph{Classifiers.}\nOur primary classifier is a Random Forest (200 trees, unconstrained depth, 5-fold stratified cross-validation).\nWe compare against CORAL ordinal regression \\cite{coral2020}, DivEye \\cite{diveye2026}, a 2-layer cross-entropy MLP, logistic regression, and a single-feature baseline using mean perplexity alone; results appear in \\S\\ref{sec:generalization}.",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Generalization and Robustness}\n\\label{sec:generalization}\n\nWe test whether the atlas results (69.6--85.4\\%) reflect genuine statistical regularities or training artifacts, evaluating four axes: classifier choice (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:baselines}), cross-model transfer (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:crossmodel}), cross-strategy transfer (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:crossstrategy}), and end-to-end learning (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:e2e}).\n\n\\subsection{Baseline Comparison}\n\\label{sec:gen:baselines}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:baselines} compares classification methods on the Pythia-1.4B nucleus setting.\nThree feature-based methods---Random Forest (RF), CORAL ordinal regression \\cite{coral2020}, and DivEye \\cite{diveye2026}---all operate on the same 19 statistical features and achieve statistically indistinguishable accuracy (78.1--78.2\\%; pairwise bootstrap $p>0.05$ after Bonferroni correction, 95\\% CI within $\\pm$1.1pp).\nThese three significantly outperform CE-MLP ($p<0.001$, $+$3.8pp), which in turn trails logistic regression by $\\sim$0.1pp.\nSingle-feature regression on mean perplexity performs at chance (25.1\\%), confirming that depth estimation requires the multi-dimensional feature representation defined in \\S\\ref{sec:method}.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Comparison of classification methods on Pythia-1.4B nucleus. RF, CORAL, and DivEye use the same 19 statistical features and are statistically indistinguishable (bootstrap $p>0.05$, Bonferroni-corrected). Chance = 25\\%. 5-fold CV std: 0.2--0.9pp across all methods.}\n\\label{tab:baselines}\n\\begin{tabular}{lc}\n\\toprule\nMethod & 4-class Acc (\\%) \\\\\n\\midrule\nRF (19 features) & 78.2 \\\\\nCORAL & 78.1 \\\\\nDivEye & 78.2 \\\\\nCE-MLP & 74.9 \\\\\nLR & 75.0 \\\\\nDeepSeek V3.2 (zero-shot)\\footnotemark & 40.0 \\\\\nppl-regression (single feature) & 25.1 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\footnotetext{Evaluated on 200 samples (approximately 50 per class) from the Pythia-1.4B nucleus cell only, due to API cost; 95\\% CI: $\\pm$6.8pp. All other methods use the full 20{,}000-sample set.}\n\\end{table}\n\nThat three structurally different classifiers achieve identical performance confirms that the discriminative signal resides in the 19 features, not the classifier architecture; a zero-shot LLM baseline (DeepSeek V3.2) reaches only 40.0\\%.\nFigure~\\ref{fig:confusion_matrix} shows d0 is near-perfectly identified in both settings; errors concentrate at the d2/d3 boundary in-distribution and spread across d1--d3 under cross-model transfer.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{../../figures/paper/fig_confusion_matrix.pdf}\n\\caption{Confusion matrices for the best in-distribution setting (GPT-2 XL, temp $\\tau{=}0.9$, 85.4\\%) and the best cross-model leave-one-out transfer (OLMo holdout, 69.2\\%). d0 recall exceeds 99\\% in both; errors spread across the d1--d3 range under transfer.}\n\\label{fig:confusion_matrix}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Model Transfer}\n\\label{sec:gen:crossmodel}\n\nWe perform leave-one-model-family-out evaluation: train on two families (nucleus only), test on the held-out third (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_model}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cross-model transfer (leave-one-out, nucleus only). Train on 2 model families, test on held-out 3rd. Chance = 25\\%.}\n\\label{tab:cross_model}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\nHoldout & Acc & d0 & d1 & d2 & d3 \\\\\n & (\\%) & Rec. & Rec. & Rec. & Rec. \\\\\n\\midrule\nPythia & 62.4 & 100.0 & 49.3 & 26.8 & 73.6 \\\\\nOLMo & \\textbf{69.2} & 99.3 & 75.5 & 43.3 & 58.8 \\\\\nGPT-2 XL & 67.6 & 98.5 & 74.7 & 52.6 & 44.5 \\\\\n\\midrule\nMean & 66.4 & 99.3 & 66.5 & 40.9 & 59.0 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nMean transfer accuracy reaches 66.4\\% (2.7$\\times$ chance), though d0 recall ($>$98\\%) inflates the headline; d2 recall drops to 26.8\\% (Pythia holdout), making it the most confusable class.\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Strategy Transfer}\n\\label{sec:gen:crossstrategy}\n\nWe train on two decoding strategies and test on the held-out third (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_strategy}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cross-strategy transfer (leave-one-out). Train on 2 strategies, test on held-out 3rd. Chance = 25\\%.}\n\\label{tab:cross_strategy}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\nHoldout & Pythia & OLMo & GPT-2 XL \\\\\n & Acc (\\%) & Acc (\\%) & Acc (\\%) \\\\\n\\midrule\nnucleus & \\textbf{69.4} & \\textbf{65.4} & \\textbf{59.9} \\\\\ntemp ($\\tau{=}0.9$) & 62.8 & 58.2 & 56.1 \\\\\ntop-$k$ ($k{=}50$) & 51.5 & 54.9 & 53.6 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nMean cross-strategy accuracy (59.1\\%) falls 7 points below cross-model transfer (66.4\\%), indicating that strategy-induced distribution shifts pose a greater generalization challenge than architectural differences.\nAs with cross-model transfer, d0 recall remains high ($\\geq$89.5\\%) across all holdout settings, inflating the headline accuracy; d2 recall ranges from 3.7\\% to 19\\%, confirming mid-chain depths as the primary confusion locus across both transfer axes.\n\n\\paragraph{Temperature-induced boundary inversion.}\nTraining on nucleus + top-$k$ and testing on temperature yields d1v2 AUC of 0.129 for GPT-2~XL and 0.253 for OLMo---both below 0.5, indicating that the d1/d2 decision boundary \\emph{inverts} (Pythia retains a degraded 0.576), consistent with the accelerated convergence in \\S\\ref{sec:atlas:strategy}.\nZ-score normalization \\cite{sugiyama2007_importance_weighting} improves logistic regression cross-strategy accuracy (25\\%$\\to$61\\%) but does not recover the inversion---all three models worsen (GPT-2~XL 0.50$\\to$0.24, OLMo 0.50$\\to$0.19, Pythia 0.63$\\to$0.52)---confirming a genuine geometry-level shift rather than a correctable scale difference.\n\n\\subsection{End-to-End Learning}\n\\label{sec:gen:e2e}\n\nWe finetune DeBERTa-v3-base \\cite{he2021_deberta_v3} and RoBERTa-base \\cite{liu2019_roberta} end-to-end (lr$\\,{=}\\,2{\\times}10^{-5}$, 3 epochs, 5-fold CV) on Pythia-1.4B nucleus data.\nBoth converge to ${\\sim}$59\\% (RoBERTa 59.5$\\pm$3.2\\%, DeBERTa 58.3$\\pm$4.7\\%), 19pp below feature-based RF (78.2\\%); the 1.2pp inter-backbone gap rules out architecture selection bias.\nBoth easily separate d0 (F1$>$0.95) but fail at adjacent-depth discrimination (d1/d2 F1$<$0.42).\nProbing reveals a negative correlation between PLM attention and RF permutation importance for both backbones (DeBERTa $\\rho{=}{-}0.484$, RoBERTa $\\rho{=}{-}0.456$; zero top-5 overlap; Appendix~\\ref{app:feature_overlap}), confirming that PLMs encode surface lexical cues while our features capture perplexity tail structure inaccessible to end-to-end learning.\n\nDCOL \\cite{cloc2025}, which adds a contrastive ordinal loss, fared worse: across six iterations with two backbones (Appendix~\\ref{app:dcol_iterations}), the best run (RoBERTa-large) reached only 64.5\\% with severe overfitting, 13.7pp below RF.\nThat all end-to-end approaches---standard finetuning (58--60\\%), DCOL (64.5\\%)---substantially underperform feature-based methods (78.2\\%) confirms that depth estimation requires explicit distributional statistics rather than learned text representations.",

    "oldString": "\\begin{equation}\n\\mathrm{EDD}_T = \\max \\{ d^*{+}1 : \\mathrm{AUC}(d^*, d^*{+}1) \\geq T \\}\n\\label{eq:edd}\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $d^* \\in \\{0, 1, \\dots, K{-}1\\}$.",
    "newString": "\\begin{equation}\n\\mathrm{EDD}_T = \\max \\bigl\\{ d^*{+}1 : \\forall\\, d' \\leq d^*,\\; \\mathrm{AUC}(d', d'{+}1) \\geq T \\bigr\\}\n\\label{eq:edd}\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $d^* \\in \\{0, 1, \\dots, K{-}1\\}$.\\footnote{In our data, pairwise AUC decreases monotonically with depth across all configurations tested, so the universal quantifier ($\\forall d' \\leq d^*$) and the simpler $\\max\\{d^*{+}1 : \\mathrm{AUC}(d^*, d^*{+}1) \\geq T\\}$ yield identical values.  The $\\forall$ formulation is preferred because it guarantees a contiguous discriminable range, which the pointwise version does not when AUC is non-monotonic.}",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Task Formulation}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\n\\subsection{Problem Definition}\n\\label{sec:method:problem}\n\nLet $f_\\theta$ denote a language model and $\\mathcal{D}_0$ a corpus of human-written seed text.\nWe define a \\emph{recursive generation chain} of depth $K$ as the sequence $\\mathcal{D}_0, \\mathcal{D}_1, \\dots, \\mathcal{D}_K$, where each $\\mathcal{D}_k$ ($k \\geq 1$) is produced by finetuning $f_\\theta$ on $\\mathcal{D}_{k-1}$ and sampling from the resulting model.\nDepth $d{=}0$ denotes human-written text; depth $d{=}k$ ($k \\geq 1$) denotes text generated by a model trained on depth-$(k{-}1)$ data.\n\nGiven a text passage $x$, the task is to estimate its recursive generation depth $d \\in \\{0, 1, \\dots, K\\}$.\nThis is an ordinal classification problem: depths carry a natural ordering, and $d{=}1$ is structurally closer to $d{=}0$ than $d{=}3$ is, since each generation step compounds distributional drift.\n\n\\subsection{Multi-Generational Benchmark Construction}\n\\label{sec:method:benchmark}\n\nWe construct a benchmark spanning three axes: model family, decoding strategy, and generation depth.\n\n\\paragraph{Model families.}\nWe select three 1--1.5B-parameter models with diverse pretraining corpora and architectures: Pythia-1.4B (EleutherAI), OLMo-1B (AI2), and GPT-2~XL (OpenAI, 1.5B).\nRestricting to a narrow parameter range isolates the effect of architecture and training data from scale.\n\n\\paragraph{Decoding strategies.}\nEach model is paired with three decoding strategies: nucleus sampling ($p{=}0.95$) \\cite{holtzman2020_nucleus}, temperature sampling ($\\tau{=}0.9$), and top-$k$ sampling ($k{=}50$).\nThese span the dominant modes of open-ended generation and induce qualitatively different entropy profiles.\n\n\\paragraph{Depth and scale.}\nThe primary benchmark covers depths d0--d3 across all $3 \\times 3 = 9$ model--strategy cells, with 5{,}000 samples per depth per cell (180K samples total).\nFor depth extension experiments (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas}), we extend one pipeline (Pythia-1.4B, nucleus) to d0--d5.\n\n\\paragraph{Generation pipeline.}\nStarting from a Wikipedia subset as seed text ($\\mathcal{D}_0$), each generation cycle proceeds in three steps: (1)~LoRA finetuning \\cite{hu2022lora} with rank $r{=}16$, $\\alpha{=}32$, learning rate $2{\\times}10^{-4}$, batch size 8, for 3 epochs; (2)~generation of 5{,}000 passages (max 512 tokens) under the specified decoding strategy; (3)~the generated corpus becomes $\\mathcal{D}_k$ and serves as training data for the next cycle.\nEach generation cycle samples unconditionally from the BOS token; there is no per-passage lineage across depths, ensuring that cross-validation splits do not introduce information leakage.\nLoRA targets are architecture-specific: \\texttt{query\\_key\\_value} for Pythia (GPT-NeoX), \\texttt{q\\_proj}/\\texttt{k\\_proj}/\\texttt{v\\_proj} for OLMo, and \\texttt{c\\_attn}/\\texttt{c\\_proj} for GPT-2~XL.\nWe adopt commonly used LoRA hyperparameters ($r{=}16$, $\\alpha{=}32$, dropout$\\,{=}\\,0.05$) following \\citet{hu2022lora}, applied uniformly across all models.\n\n\\paragraph{Reference model.}\nAll statistical features (\\S\\ref{sec:method:features}) are computed using a fixed reference model---GPT-2 base (124M)---that is independent of the three generation models.\nThe reference model computes per-token perplexity for each passage, providing a consistent measurement frame across model families.\nIn cross-model leave-one-out evaluation (\\S\\ref{sec:generalization}), the reference model remains fixed even when GPT-2~XL is held out; the partial architectural overlap this introduces is discussed in Limitations.\n\n\\subsection{Statistical Feature Design}\n\\label{sec:method:features}\n\nWe extract a 19-dimensional feature vector $\\mathbf{f}(x)$ from the reference-model perplexity distribution over each passage $x$.\nThe features fall into four categories (Table~\\ref{tab:features}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{The 19 statistical features comprising $\\mathbf{f}(x)$, grouped by category. All perplexity and surprisal features are computed from the reference model (GPT-2 base, 124M).}\n\\label{tab:features}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llr@{}}\n\\toprule\nCategory & Features & $n$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nPerplexity & mean, var, skewness, kurtosis, & 9 \\\\\nstatistics & p10, p25, p50, p75, p90 & \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nSurprisal & mean, var, entropy & 3 \\\\\nstatistics & & \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nLexical & type--token ratio, hapax ratio, & 4 \\\\\ndiversity & bigram entropy, trigram entropy & \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nRepetition & repeated 2/3/4-gram fractions & 3 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\emph{Perplexity statistics} (9 features) capture the location, spread, and shape of the per-token perplexity distribution via its mean, variance, skewness, kurtosis, and five percentile quantiles (p10--p90).\nThe tail quantiles (p75, p90) prove especially informative: recursive finetuning erodes the high-perplexity tail, making these features monotonically decreasing with depth (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas}).\n\n\\emph{Surprisal statistics} (3 features) operate on $-\\log p(\\text{token})$ and include mean surprisal, surprisal variance, and the entropy of the surprisal distribution.\nWhile correlated with perplexity features, surprisal variance captures second-order structure---the variability of token predictability---that is not redundant with perplexity moments.\n\n\\emph{Lexical diversity} (4 features) measures vocabulary usage through type--token ratio, hapax ratio (fraction of tokens appearing exactly once), and bigram/trigram entropy.\n\n\\emph{Repetition} (3 features) quantifies the fraction of repeated $n$-grams for $n \\in \\{2, 3, 4\\}$, capturing the increased self-repetition characteristic of recursive generation.\n\n\\subsection{Effective Discriminable Depth}\n\\label{sec:method:edd}\n\nAggregate classification accuracy over $K{+}1$ classes obscures whether discriminability degrades at specific depths.\nWe introduce the \\emph{Effective Discriminable Depth} (EDD), a scalar metric that summarizes how far into a recursive chain adjacent depths remain distinguishable.\n\nFor each adjacent depth pair $(d, d{+}1)$, we train a dedicated binary Random Forest (500 trees; increased from 200 in the multi-class setting to ensure convergence on the smaller per-pair samples) and compute the one-vs-one AUC.\nGiven a threshold $T$, we define:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\mathrm{EDD}_T = \\max \\{ d^*{+}1 : \\mathrm{AUC}(d^*, d^*{+}1) \\geq T \\}\n\\label{eq:edd}\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $d^* \\in \\{0, 1, \\dots, K{-}1\\}$.\nThe default threshold is $T{=}0.75$; we report sensitivity analyses at $T \\in \\{0.65, 0.70, 0.75, 0.80\\}$ in \\S\\ref{sec:atlas}.\nCrucially, each AUC is obtained from an independent binary pairwise classifier (5-fold stratified CV) rather than extracted from a single multi-class model, avoiding decision boundary dilution (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas}).\n\nEDD captures a natural question: \\emph{how deep into the recursive chain can we reliably distinguish successive generations?}\nA high EDD indicates that the statistical fingerprints retain discriminative power across many recursion steps; a low EDD signals rapid convergence to an indistinguishable regime.\n\n\\paragraph{Classifiers.}\nOur primary classifier is a Random Forest (200 trees, unconstrained depth, 5-fold stratified cross-validation).\nWe compare against CORAL ordinal regression \\cite{coral2020}, DivEye \\cite{diveye2026}, a 2-layer cross-entropy MLP, logistic regression, and a single-feature baseline using mean perplexity alone; results appear in \\S\\ref{sec:generalization}.",

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01HN6m41N8Rkp7sJ6ddDmM36`</summary>

```text
25	\paragraph{EDD threshold sensitivity.}
26	The Effective Discriminable Depth depends on the AUC threshold $T$: at $T{=}0.80$, several cells lose one discriminable depth level relative to $T{=}0.75$.
27	The choice of $T$ is application-dependent and affects reported EDD values.
28	
29	\paragraph{Reference model dependency.}
30	Surprisal-based features are computed relative to GPT-2 base (124M), introducing partial overlap when GPT-2~XL is held out, though GPT-2~XL holdout accuracy (67.6\%) does not exceed the unrelated OLMo (69.2\%).
31	The choice of reference model affects feature values; a different reference model may shift absolute feature magnitudes and potentially alter discriminability at depth boundaries.
32	
33	\paragraph{Additional caveats.}
34	Detection features degrade under domain shift \cite{pudasaini2026_detection_fails}; our depth features are likely susceptible.
35	\citet{bakshi2026_collapse_improvement} find that early recursion can temporarily \emph{improve} model quality, potentially violating the monotonicity assumption, and \citet{falahati2026_curated} show that curated synthetic data need not trigger collapse, attenuating the fingerprints we exploit.
```

</details>

    "originalFile": "\\section{Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nWe introduced recursive generation depth estimation and showed that 19 lightweight statistical features---with p90 perplexity and surprisal variance as universal markers---achieve 70--85\\% four-class accuracy (chance = 25\\%) with $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}{=}4$.\nCross-model transfer reaches 66.4\\% and cross-strategy 59.1\\%, though the temperature-induced d1/d2 inversion and PLMs' ${\\sim}$59\\% ceiling underscore remaining challenges; future work should address wild-data transfer, larger scales, and strategy-conditioned classifiers.\nCode and data will be released upon acceptance.\n\n\\section*{Limitations}\n\\label{sec:limitations}\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-strategy deployment constraint.}\nDeploying depth estimators across decoding strategies requires knowing the target strategy type a priori.\nWithout this knowledge, d1/d2 predictions may systematically invert: cross-strategy holdout d1v2 AUC drops to 0.129 (GPT-2~XL) and 0.253 (OLMo) when temperature-sampled text is classified by a model trained on nucleus and top-$k$ (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:crossstrategy}).\nStrategy-conditioned classifiers or automatic strategy identification are needed before deployment.\n\n\\paragraph{d0 inflation of headline metrics.}\nDepth~0 (human-written text) is near-perfectly separable (recall $>$98\\% across all settings), inflating headline accuracy.\nCross-model transfer accuracy of 66.4\\% is substantially driven by d0; d2 recall drops to 26.8\\% in the worst case (Pythia holdout).\n\n\\paragraph{Controlled vs.\\ ecological setting.}\nAll experiments use Wikipedia seed text with controlled recursive generation (LoRA fine-tuning, 1--1.5B parameter models).\nTransfer to mixed-domain corpora, instruction-tuned models, and larger scales (7B+) remains open.\nWild-data calibration is probed only indirectly; ecological transfer is future work.\n\n\\paragraph{EDD threshold sensitivity.}\nThe Effective Discriminable Depth depends on the AUC threshold $T$: at $T{=}0.80$, several cells lose one discriminable depth level relative to $T{=}0.75$.\nThe choice of $T$ is application-dependent and affects reported EDD values.\n\n\\paragraph{Reference model dependency.}\nSurprisal-based features are computed relative to GPT-2 base (124M), introducing partial overlap when GPT-2~XL is held out, though GPT-2~XL holdout accuracy (67.6\\%) does not exceed the unrelated OLMo (69.2\\%).\nThe choice of reference model affects feature values; a different reference model may shift absolute feature magnitudes and potentially alter discriminability at depth boundaries.\n\n\\paragraph{Additional caveats.}\nDetection features degrade under domain shift \\cite{pudasaini2026_detection_fails}; our depth features are likely susceptible.\n\\citet{bakshi2026_collapse_improvement} find that early recursion can temporarily \\emph{improve} model quality, potentially violating the monotonicity assumption, and \\citet{falahati2026_curated} show that curated synthetic data need not trigger collapse, attenuating the fingerprints we exploit.",

    "oldString": "\\paragraph{Additional caveats.}\nDetection features degrade under domain shift \\cite{pudasaini2026_detection_fails}; our depth features are likely susceptible.\n\\citet{bakshi2026_collapse_improvement} find that early recursion can temporarily \\emph{improve} model quality, potentially violating the monotonicity assumption, and \\citet{falahati2026_curated} show that curated synthetic data need not trigger collapse, attenuating the fingerprints we exploit.",
    "newString": "\\paragraph{Additional caveats.}\nDetection features degrade under domain shift \\cite{pudasaini2026_detection_fails}; our depth features are likely susceptible.\n\\citet{falahati2026_curated} show that curated synthetic data need not trigger collapse, attenuating the fingerprints we exploit.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nWe introduced recursive generation depth estimation and showed that 19 lightweight statistical features---with p90 perplexity and surprisal variance as universal markers---achieve 70--85\\% four-class accuracy (chance = 25\\%) with $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}{=}4$.\nCross-model transfer reaches 66.4\\% and cross-strategy 59.1\\%, though the temperature-induced d1/d2 inversion and PLMs' ${\\sim}$59\\% ceiling underscore remaining challenges; future work should address wild-data transfer, larger scales, and strategy-conditioned classifiers.\nCode and data will be released upon acceptance.\n\n\\section*{Limitations}\n\\label{sec:limitations}\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-strategy deployment constraint.}\nDeploying depth estimators across decoding strategies requires knowing the target strategy type a priori.\nWithout this knowledge, d1/d2 predictions may systematically invert: cross-strategy holdout d1v2 AUC drops to 0.129 (GPT-2~XL) and 0.253 (OLMo) when temperature-sampled text is classified by a model trained on nucleus and top-$k$ (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:crossstrategy}).\nStrategy-conditioned classifiers or automatic strategy identification are needed before deployment.\n\n\\paragraph{d0 inflation of headline metrics.}\nDepth~0 (human-written text) is near-perfectly separable (recall $>$98\\% across all settings), inflating headline accuracy.\nCross-model transfer accuracy of 66.4\\% is substantially driven by d0; d2 recall drops to 26.8\\% in the worst case (Pythia holdout).\n\n\\paragraph{Controlled vs.\\ ecological setting.}\nAll experiments use Wikipedia seed text with controlled recursive generation (LoRA fine-tuning, 1--1.5B parameter models).\nTransfer to mixed-domain corpora, instruction-tuned models, and larger scales (7B+) remains open.\nWild-data calibration is probed only indirectly; ecological transfer is future work.\n\n\\paragraph{EDD monotonicity assumption.}\nOur EDD definition (Eq.~\\ref{eq:edd}) requires all pairwise AUCs up to depth $d^*$ to exceed $T$, which is well-behaved when AUC decays monotonically---as observed in all our configurations.\nHowever, \\citet{bakshi2026_collapse_improvement} show that early recursion can temporarily \\emph{improve} model quality, which could produce non-monotonic AUC profiles (e.g., a dip at d1/d2 followed by recovery at d2/d3).\nIn such cases EDD would conservatively report a lower value than the deepest discriminable pair, potentially underestimating the true discriminable range; extending EDD to handle non-contiguous discriminable regions is future work.\nThe choice of threshold $T$ is also application-dependent: at $T{=}0.80$, several cells lose one depth level relative to $T{=}0.75$.\n\n\\paragraph{Reference model dependency.}\nSurprisal-based features are computed relative to GPT-2 base (124M), introducing partial overlap when GPT-2~XL is held out, though GPT-2~XL holdout accuracy (67.6\\%) does not exceed the unrelated OLMo (69.2\\%).\nThe choice of reference model affects feature values; a different reference model may shift absolute feature magnitudes and potentially alter discriminability at depth boundaries.\n\n\\paragraph{Additional caveats.}\nDetection features degrade under domain shift \\cite{pudasaini2026_detection_fails}; our depth features are likely susceptible.\n\\citet{bakshi2026_collapse_improvement} find that early recursion can temporarily \\emph{improve} model quality, potentially violating the monotonicity assumption, and \\citet{falahati2026_curated} show that curated synthetic data need not trigger collapse, attenuating the fingerprints we exploit.",

Let me look at what changed in the Limitations section—I expanded the EDD paragraph and moved some content there, so I should calculate the net text change to see where I can trim. The monotonicity assumption breaks down when early recursion temporarily improves model quality, causing non-monotonic AUC profiles that would make EDD underestimate the true discriminable range—handling non-contiguous regions remains future work. Our depth features likely degrade under domain shift just as detection features do, and while curated synthetic data can avoid triggering collapse entirely, this attenuates the fingerprints we're trying to exploit. ```text
88	Given a threshold $T$, we define:
89	\begin{equation}
90	\mathrm{EDD}_T = \max \bigl\{ d^*{+}1 : \forall\, d' \leq d^*,\; \mathrm{AUC}(d', d'{+}1) \geq T \bigr\}
91	\label{eq:edd}
92	\end{equation}
93	where $d^* \in \{0, 1, \dots, K{-}1\}$.\footnote{In our data, pairwise AUC decreases monotonically with depth across all configurations tested, so the universal quantifier ($\forall d' \leq d^*$) and the simpler $\max\{d^*{+}1 : \mathrm{AUC}(d^*, d^*{+}1) \geq T\}$ yield identical values.  The $\forall$ formulation is preferred because it guarantees a contiguous discriminable range, which the pointwise version does not when AUC is non-monotonic.}
94	The default threshold is $T{=}0.75$; we report sensitivity analyses at $T \in \{0.65, 0.70, 0.75, 0.80\}$ in \S\ref{sec:atlas}.
95	Crucially, each AUC is obtained from an independent binary pairwise classifier (5-fold stratified CV) rather than extracted from a single multi-class model, avoiding decision boundary dilution (\S\ref{sec:atlas}).
```

The $\\forall$ formulation is preferred because it guarantees a contiguous discriminable range, which the pointwise version does not when AUC is non-monotonic.}",
    "newString": "where $d^* \\in \\{0, 1, \\dots, K{-}1\\}$.\\footnote{Our AUC profiles are monotonically decreasing, so the $\\forall$ and pointwise $\\max\\{d^*{+}1 : \\mathrm{AUC}(d^*,d^*{+}1) \\geq T\\}$ formulations coincide; the $\\forall$ version is preferred as it guarantees a contiguous discriminable range (see Limitations).}",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Task Formulation}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\n\\subsection{Problem Definition}\n\\label{sec:method:problem}\n\nLet $f_\\theta$ denote a language model and $\\mathcal{D}_0$ a corpus of human-written seed text.\nWe define a \\emph{recursive generation chain} of depth $K$ as the sequence $\\mathcal{D}_0, \\mathcal{D}_1, \\dots, \\mathcal{D}_K$, where each $\\mathcal{D}_k$ ($k \\geq 1$) is produced by finetuning $f_\\theta$ on $\\mathcal{D}_{k-1}$ and sampling from the resulting model.\nDepth $d{=}0$ denotes human-written text; depth $d{=}k$ ($k \\geq 1$) denotes text generated by a model trained on depth-$(k{-}1)$ data.\n\nGiven a text passage $x$, the task is to estimate its recursive generation depth $d \\in \\{0, 1, \\dots, K\\}$.\nThis is an ordinal classification problem: depths carry a natural ordering, and $d{=}1$ is structurally closer to $d{=}0$ than $d{=}3$ is, since each generation step compounds distributional drift.\n\n\\subsection{Multi-Generational Benchmark Construction}\n\\label{sec:method:benchmark}\n\nWe construct a benchmark spanning three axes: model family, decoding strategy, and generation depth.\n\n\\paragraph{Model families.}\nWe select three 1--1.5B-parameter models with diverse pretraining corpora and architectures: Pythia-1.4B (EleutherAI), OLMo-1B (AI2), and GPT-2~XL (OpenAI, 1.5B).\nRestricting to a narrow parameter range isolates the effect of architecture and training data from scale.\n\n\\paragraph{Decoding strategies.}\nEach model is paired with three decoding strategies: nucleus sampling ($p{=}0.95$) \\cite{holtzman2020_nucleus}, temperature sampling ($\\tau{=}0.9$), and top-$k$ sampling ($k{=}50$).\nThese span the dominant modes of open-ended generation and induce qualitatively different entropy profiles.\n\n\\paragraph{Depth and scale.}\nThe primary benchmark covers depths d0--d3 across all $3 \\times 3 = 9$ model--strategy cells, with 5{,}000 samples per depth per cell (180K samples total).\nFor depth extension experiments (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas}), we extend one pipeline (Pythia-1.4B, nucleus) to d0--d5.\n\n\\paragraph{Generation pipeline.}\nStarting from a Wikipedia subset as seed text ($\\mathcal{D}_0$), each generation cycle proceeds in three steps: (1)~LoRA finetuning \\cite{hu2022lora} with rank $r{=}16$, $\\alpha{=}32$, learning rate $2{\\times}10^{-4}$, batch size 8, for 3 epochs; (2)~generation of 5{,}000 passages (max 512 tokens) under the specified decoding strategy; (3)~the generated corpus becomes $\\mathcal{D}_k$ and serves as training data for the next cycle.\nEach generation cycle samples unconditionally from the BOS token; there is no per-passage lineage across depths, ensuring that cross-validation splits do not introduce information leakage.\nLoRA targets are architecture-specific: \\texttt{query\\_key\\_value} for Pythia (GPT-NeoX), \\texttt{q\\_proj}/\\texttt{k\\_proj}/\\texttt{v\\_proj} for OLMo, and \\texttt{c\\_attn}/\\texttt{c\\_proj} for GPT-2~XL.\nWe adopt commonly used LoRA hyperparameters ($r{=}16$, $\\alpha{=}32$, dropout$\\,{=}\\,0.05$) following \\citet{hu2022lora}, applied uniformly across all models.\n\n\\paragraph{Reference model.}\nAll statistical features (\\S\\ref{sec:method:features}) are computed using a fixed reference model---GPT-2 base (124M)---that is independent of the three generation models.\nThe reference model computes per-token perplexity for each passage, providing a consistent measurement frame across model families.\nIn cross-model leave-one-out evaluation (\\S\\ref{sec:generalization}), the reference model remains fixed even when GPT-2~XL is held out; the partial architectural overlap this introduces is discussed in Limitations.\n\n\\subsection{Statistical Feature Design}\n\\label{sec:method:features}\n\nWe extract a 19-dimensional feature vector $\\mathbf{f}(x)$ from the reference-model perplexity distribution over each passage $x$.\nThe features fall into four categories (Table~\\ref{tab:features}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{The 19 statistical features comprising $\\mathbf{f}(x)$, grouped by category. All perplexity and surprisal features are computed from the reference model (GPT-2 base, 124M).}\n\\label{tab:features}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llr@{}}\n\\toprule\nCategory & Features & $n$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nPerplexity & mean, var, skewness, kurtosis, & 9 \\\\\nstatistics & p10, p25, p50, p75, p90 & \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nSurprisal & mean, var, entropy & 3 \\\\\nstatistics & & \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nLexical & type--token ratio, hapax ratio, & 4 \\\\\ndiversity & bigram entropy, trigram entropy & \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nRepetition & repeated 2/3/4-gram fractions & 3 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\emph{Perplexity statistics} (9 features) capture the location, spread, and shape of the per-token perplexity distribution via its mean, variance, skewness, kurtosis, and five percentile quantiles (p10--p90).\nThe tail quantiles (p75, p90) prove especially informative: recursive finetuning erodes the high-perplexity tail, making these features monotonically decreasing with depth (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas}).\n\n\\emph{Surprisal statistics} (3 features) operate on $-\\log p(\\text{token})$ and include mean surprisal, surprisal variance, and the entropy of the surprisal distribution.\nWhile correlated with perplexity features, surprisal variance captures second-order structure---the variability of token predictability---that is not redundant with perplexity moments.\n\n\\emph{Lexical diversity} (4 features) measures vocabulary usage through type--token ratio, hapax ratio (fraction of tokens appearing exactly once), and bigram/trigram entropy.\n\n\\emph{Repetition} (3 features) quantifies the fraction of repeated $n$-grams for $n \\in \\{2, 3, 4\\}$, capturing the increased self-repetition characteristic of recursive generation.\n\n\\subsection{Effective Discriminable Depth}\n\\label{sec:method:edd}\n\nAggregate classification accuracy over $K{+}1$ classes obscures whether discriminability degrades at specific depths.\nWe introduce the \\emph{Effective Discriminable Depth} (EDD), a scalar metric that summarizes how far into a recursive chain adjacent depths remain distinguishable.\n\nFor each adjacent depth pair $(d, d{+}1)$, we train a dedicated binary Random Forest (500 trees; increased from 200 in the multi-class setting to ensure convergence on the smaller per-pair samples) and compute the one-vs-one AUC.\nGiven a threshold $T$, we define:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\mathrm{EDD}_T = \\max \\bigl\\{ d^*{+}1 : \\forall\\, d' \\leq d^*,\\; \\mathrm{AUC}(d', d'{+}1) \\geq T \\bigr\\}\n\\label{eq:edd}\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $d^* \\in \\{0, 1, \\dots, K{-}1\\}$.\\footnote{In our data, pairwise AUC decreases monotonically with depth across all configurations tested, so the universal quantifier ($\\forall d' \\leq d^*$) and the simpler $\\max\\{d^*{+}1 : \\mathrm{AUC}(d^*, d^*{+}1) \\geq T\\}$ yield identical values.  The $\\forall$ formulation is preferred because it guarantees a contiguous discriminable range, which the pointwise version does not when AUC is non-monotonic.}\nThe default threshold is $T{=}0.75$; we report sensitivity analyses at $T \\in \\{0.65, 0.70, 0.75, 0.80\\}$ in \\S\\ref{sec:atlas}.\nCrucially, each AUC is obtained from an independent binary pairwise classifier (5-fold stratified CV) rather than extracted from a single multi-class model, avoiding decision boundary dilution (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas}).\n\nEDD captures a natural question: \\emph{how deep into the recursive chain can we reliably distinguish successive generations?}\nA high EDD indicates that the statistical fingerprints retain discriminative power across many recursion steps; a low EDD signals rapid convergence to an indistinguishable regime.\n\n\\paragraph{Classifiers.}\nOur primary classifier is a Random Forest (200 trees, unconstrained depth, 5-fold stratified cross-validation).\nWe compare against CORAL ordinal regression \\cite{coral2020}, DivEye \\cite{diveye2026}, a 2-layer cross-entropy MLP, logistic regression, and a single-feature baseline using mean perplexity alone; results appear in \\S\\ref{sec:generalization}.",

4	\paragraph{Depth-graduated data curation.}

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_018tuFKNtdCiz5LGoW2X5qWT`</summary>

```text
96	\paragraph{Temperature-induced boundary inversion.}
97	Training on nucleus + top-$k$ and testing on temperature yields d1v2 AUC of 0.129 for GPT-2~XL and 0.253 for OLMo---both below 0.5, indicating that the d1/d2 decision boundary \emph{inverts} (Pythia retains a degraded 0.576), consistent with the accelerated convergence in \S\ref{sec:atlas:strategy}.
98	Z-score normalization \cite{sugiyama2007_importance_weighting} improves logistic regression cross-strategy accuracy (25\%$\to$61\%) but does not recover the inversion---all three models worsen (GPT-2~XL 0.50$\to$0.24, OLMo 0.50$\to$0.19, Pythia 0.63$\to$0.52)---confirming a genuine geometry-level shift rather than a correctable scale difference.
99	
100	\subsection{End-to-End Learning}
101	\label{sec:gen:e2e}
102	
103	We finetune DeBERTa-v3-base \cite{he2021_deberta_v3} and RoBERTa-base \cite{liu2019_roberta} end-to-end (lr$\,{=}\,2{\times}10^{-5}$, 3 epochs, 5-fold CV) on Pythia-1.4B nucleus data.
104	Both converge to ${\sim}$59\% (RoBERTa 59.5$\pm$3.2\%, DeBERTa 58.3$\pm$4.7\%), 19pp below feature-based RF (78.2\%); the 1.2pp inter-backbone gap rules out architecture selection bias.
105	Both easily separate d0 (F1$>$0.95) but fail at adjacent-depth discrimination (d1/d2 F1$<$0.42).
106	Probing reveals a negative correlation between PLM attention and RF permutation importance for both backbones (DeBERTa $\rho{=}{-}0.484$, RoBERTa $\rho{=}{-}0.456$; zero top-5 overlap; Appendix~\ref{app:feature_overlap}), confirming that PLMs encode surface lexical cues while our features capture perplexity tail structure inaccessible to end-to-end learning.
107	
108	DCOL \cite{cloc2025}, which adds a contrastive ordinal loss, fared worse: across six iterations with two backbones (Appendix~\ref{app:dcol_iterations}), the best run (RoBERTa-large) reached only 64.5\% with severe overfitting, 13.7pp below RF.
109	That all end-to-end approaches---standard finetuning (58--60\%), DCOL (64.5\%)---substantially underperform feature-based methods (78.2\%) confirms that depth estimation requires explicit distributional statistics rather than learned text representations.
```

    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513_2/projects/recursive_gen_depth_est/docs/paper/generalization.tex",
    "oldString": "DCOL \\cite{cloc2025}, which adds a contrastive ordinal loss, fared worse: across six iterations with two backbones (Appendix~\\ref{app:dcol_iterations}), the best run (RoBERTa-large) reached only 64.5\\% with severe overfitting, 13.7pp below RF.\nThat all end-to-end approaches---standard finetuning (58--60\\%), DCOL (64.5\\%)---substantially underperform feature-based methods (78.2\\%) confirms that depth estimation requires explicit distributional statistics rather than learned text representations.",
    "newString": "DCOL \\cite{cloc2025}, which adds a contrastive ordinal loss, reached only 64.5\\% in its best configuration (RoBERTa-large; Appendix~\\ref{app:dcol_iterations}), 13.7pp below RF.\nThat all end-to-end approaches---finetuning (58--60\\%), DCOL (64.5\\%)---substantially underperform feature-based methods (78.2\\%) confirms that depth estimation requires explicit distributional statistics rather than learned text representations.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Generalization and Robustness}\n\\label{sec:generalization}\n\nWe test whether the atlas results (69.6--85.4\\%) reflect genuine statistical regularities or training artifacts, evaluating four axes: classifier choice (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:baselines}), cross-model transfer (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:crossmodel}), cross-strategy transfer (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:crossstrategy}), and end-to-end learning (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:e2e}).\n\n\\subsection{Baseline Comparison}\n\\label{sec:gen:baselines}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:baselines} compares classification methods on the Pythia-1.4B nucleus setting.\nThree feature-based methods---Random Forest (RF), CORAL ordinal regression \\cite{coral2020}, and DivEye \\cite{diveye2026}---all operate on the same 19 statistical features and achieve statistically indistinguishable accuracy (78.1--78.2\\%; pairwise bootstrap $p>0.05$ after Bonferroni correction, 95\\% CI within $\\pm$1.1pp).\nThese three significantly outperform CE-MLP ($p<0.001$, $+$3.8pp), which in turn trails logistic regression by $\\sim$0.1pp.\nSingle-feature regression on mean perplexity performs at chance (25.1\\%), confirming that depth estimation requires the multi-dimensional feature representation defined in \\S\\ref{sec:method}.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Comparison of classification methods on Pythia-1.4B nucleus. RF, CORAL, and DivEye use the same 19 statistical features and are statistically indistinguishable (bootstrap $p>0.05$, Bonferroni-corrected). Chance = 25\\%. 5-fold CV std: 0.2--0.9pp across all methods.}\n\\label{tab:baselines}\n\\begin{tabular}{lc}\n\\toprule\nMethod & 4-class Acc (\\%) \\\\\n\\midrule\nRF (19 features) & 78.2 \\\\\nCORAL & 78.1 \\\\\nDivEye & 78.2 \\\\\nCE-MLP & 74.9 \\\\\nLR & 75.0 \\\\\nDeepSeek V3.2 (zero-shot)\\footnotemark & 40.0 \\\\\nppl-regression (single feature) & 25.1 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\footnotetext{Evaluated on 200 samples (approximately 50 per class) from the Pythia-1.4B nucleus cell only, due to API cost; 95\\% CI: $\\pm$6.8pp. All other methods use the full 20{,}000-sample set.}\n\\end{table}\n\nThat three structurally different classifiers achieve identical performance confirms that the discriminative signal resides in the 19 features, not the classifier architecture; a zero-shot LLM baseline (DeepSeek V3.2) reaches only 40.0\\%.\nFigure~\\ref{fig:confusion_matrix} shows d0 is near-perfectly identified in both settings; errors concentrate at the d2/d3 boundary in-distribution and spread across d1--d3 under cross-model transfer.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_confusion_matrix.pdf}\n\\caption{Confusion matrices for the best in-distribution setting (GPT-2 XL, temp $\\tau{=}0.9$, 85.4\\%) and the best cross-model leave-one-out transfer (OLMo holdout, 69.2\\%). d0 recall exceeds 99\\% in both; errors spread across the d1--d3 range under transfer.}\n\\label{fig:confusion_matrix}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Model Transfer}\n\\label{sec:gen:crossmodel}\n\nWe perform leave-one-model-family-out evaluation: train on two families (nucleus only), test on the held-out third (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_model}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cross-model transfer (leave-one-out, nucleus only). Train on 2 model families, test on held-out 3rd. Chance = 25\\%.}\n\\label{tab:cross_model}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\nHoldout & Acc & d0 & d1 & d2 & d3 \\\\\n & (\\%) & Rec. & Rec. & Rec. & Rec. \\\\\n\\midrule\nPythia & 62.4 & 100.0 & 49.3 & 26.8 & 73.6 \\\\\nOLMo & \\textbf{69.2} & 99.3 & 75.5 & 43.3 & 58.8 \\\\\nGPT-2 XL & 67.6 & 98.5 & 74.7 & 52.6 & 44.5 \\\\\n\\midrule\nMean & 66.4 & 99.3 & 66.5 & 40.9 & 59.0 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nMean transfer accuracy reaches 66.4\\% (2.7$\\times$ chance), though d0 recall ($>$98\\%) inflates the headline; d2 recall drops to 26.8\\% (Pythia holdout), making it the most confusable class.\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Strategy Transfer}\n\\label{sec:gen:crossstrategy}\n\nWe train on two decoding strategies and test on the held-out third (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_strategy}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cross-strategy transfer (leave-one-out). Train on 2 strategies, test on held-out 3rd. Chance = 25\\%.}\n\\label{tab:cross_strategy}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\nHoldout & Pythia & OLMo & GPT-2 XL \\\\\n & Acc (\\%) & Acc (\\%) & Acc (\\%) \\\\\n\\midrule\nnucleus & \\textbf{69.4} & \\textbf{65.4} & \\textbf{59.9} \\\\\ntemp ($\\tau{=}0.9$) & 62.8 & 58.2 & 56.1 \\\\\ntop-$k$ ($k{=}50$) & 51.5 & 54.9 & 53.6 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nMean cross-strategy accuracy (59.1\\%) falls 7 points below cross-model transfer (66.4\\%), indicating that strategy-induced distribution shifts pose a greater generalization challenge than architectural differences.\nAs with cross-model transfer, d0 recall remains high ($\\geq$89.5\\%) across all holdout settings, inflating the headline accuracy; d2 recall ranges from 3.7\\% to 19\\%, confirming mid-chain depths as the primary confusion locus across both transfer axes.\n\n\\paragraph{Temperature-induced boundary inversion.}\nTraining on nucleus + top-$k$ and testing on temperature yields d1v2 AUC of 0.129 for GPT-2~XL and 0.253 for OLMo---both below 0.5, indicating that the d1/d2 decision boundary \\emph{inverts} (Pythia retains a degraded 0.576), consistent with the accelerated convergence in \\S\\ref{sec:atlas:strategy}.\nZ-score normalization \\cite{sugiyama2007_importance_weighting} improves logistic regression cross-strategy accuracy (25\\%$\\to$61\\%) but does not recover the inversion---all three models worsen (GPT-2~XL 0.50$\\to$0.24, OLMo 0.50$\\to$0.19, Pythia 0.63$\\to$0.52)---confirming a genuine geometry-level shift rather than a correctable scale difference.\n\n\\subsection{End-to-End Learning}\n\\label{sec:gen:e2e}\n\nWe finetune DeBERTa-v3-base \\cite{he2021_deberta_v3} and RoBERTa-base \\cite{liu2019_roberta} end-to-end (lr$\\,{=}\\,2{\\times}10^{-5}$, 3 epochs, 5-fold CV) on Pythia-1.4B nucleus data.\nBoth converge to ${\\sim}$59\\% (RoBERTa 59.5$\\pm$3.2\\%, DeBERTa 58.3$\\pm$4.7\\%), 19pp below feature-based RF (78.2\\%); the 1.2pp inter-backbone gap rules out architecture selection bias.\nBoth easily separate d0 (F1$>$0.95) but fail at adjacent-depth discrimination (d1/d2 F1$<$0.42).\nProbing reveals a negative correlation between PLM attention and RF permutation importance for both backbones (DeBERTa $\\rho{=}{-}0.484$, RoBERTa $\\rho{=}{-}0.456$; zero top-5 overlap; Appendix~\\ref{app:feature_overlap}), confirming that PLMs encode surface lexical cues while our features capture perplexity tail structure inaccessible to end-to-end learning.\n\nDCOL \\cite{cloc2025}, which adds a contrastive ordinal loss, fared worse: across six iterations with two backbones (Appendix~\\ref{app:dcol_iterations}), the best run (RoBERTa-large) reached only 64.5\\% with severe overfitting, 13.7pp below RF.\nThat all end-to-end approaches---standard finetuning (58--60\\%), DCOL (64.5\\%)---substantially underperform feature-based methods (78.2\\%) confirms that depth estimation requires explicit distributional statistics rather than learned text representations.",

89	\bottomrule
90	\end{tabular}

92	
93	Mean cross-strategy accuracy (59.1\%) falls 7 points below cross-model transfer (66.4\%), indicating that strategy-induced distribution shifts pose a greater generalization challenge than architectural differences.
94	As with cross-model transfer, d0 recall remains high ($\geq$89.5\%) across all holdout settings, inflating the headline accuracy; d2 recall ranges from 3.7\% to 19\%, confirming mid-chain depths as the primary confusion locus across both transfer axes.
95	
96	\paragraph{Temperature-induced boundary inversion.}
97	Training on nucleus + top-$k$ and testing on temperature yields d1v2 AUC of 0.129 for GPT-2~XL and 0.253 for OLMo---both below 0.5, indicating that the d1/d2 decision boundary \emph{inverts} (Pythia retains a degraded 0.576), consistent with the accelerated convergence in \S\ref{sec:atlas:strategy}.
98	Z-score normalization \cite{sugiyama2007_importance_weighting} improves logistic regression cross-strategy accuracy (25\%$\to$61\%) but does not recover the inversion---all three models worsen (GPT-2~XL 0.50$\to$0.24, OLMo 0.50$\to$0.19, Pythia 0.63$\to$0.52)---confirming a genuine geometry-level shift rather than a correctable scale difference.
99
