  "toolUseResult": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513_2/projects/recursive_gen_depth_est/docs/paper/generalization.tex",
    "oldString": "Mean cross-strategy accuracy (59.1\\%) falls 7 points below cross-model transfer (66.4\\%), indicating that strategy-induced distribution shifts pose a greater generalization challenge than architectural differences.\nAs with cross-model transfer, d0 recall remains high ($\\geq$89.5\\%) across all holdout settings, inflating the headline accuracy; d2 recall ranges from 3.7\\% to 19\\%, confirming mid-chain depths as the primary confusion locus across both transfer axes.",
    "newString": "Mean cross-strategy accuracy (59.1\\%) falls 7pp below cross-model transfer (66.4\\%), indicating that strategy-induced shifts pose a greater challenge than architectural differences.\nAs with cross-model transfer, d0 recall remains high ($\\geq$89.5\\%), inflating headline accuracy; d2 recall ranges from 3.7\\% to 19\\%, confirming mid-chain depths as the primary confusion locus.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Generalization and Robustness}\n\\label{sec:generalization}\n\nWe test whether the atlas results (69.6--85.4\\%) reflect genuine statistical regularities or training artifacts, evaluating four axes: classifier choice (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:baselines}), cross-model transfer (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:crossmodel}), cross-strategy transfer (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:crossstrategy}), and end-to-end learning (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:e2e}).\n\n\\subsection{Baseline Comparison}\n\\label{sec:gen:baselines}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:baselines} compares classification methods on the Pythia-1.4B nucleus setting.\nThree feature-based methods---Random Forest (RF), CORAL ordinal regression \\cite{coral2020}, and DivEye \\cite{diveye2026}---all operate on the same 19 statistical features and achieve statistically indistinguishable accuracy (78.1--78.2\\%; pairwise bootstrap $p>0.05$ after Bonferroni correction, 95\\% CI within $\\pm$1.1pp).\nThese three significantly outperform CE-MLP ($p<0.001$, $+$3.8pp), which in turn trails logistic regression by $\\sim$0.1pp.\nSingle-feature regression on mean perplexity performs at chance (25.1\\%), confirming that depth estimation requires the multi-dimensional feature representation defined in \\S\\ref{sec:method}.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Comparison of classification methods on Pythia-1.4B nucleus. RF, CORAL, and DivEye use the same 19 statistical features and are statistically indistinguishable (bootstrap $p>0.05$, Bonferroni-corrected). Chance = 25\\%. 5-fold CV std: 0.2--0.9pp across all methods.}\n\\label{tab:baselines}\n\\begin{tabular}{lc}\n\\toprule\nMethod & 4-class Acc (\\%) \\\\\n\\midrule\nRF (19 features) & 78.2 \\\\\nCORAL & 78.1 \\\\\nDivEye & 78.2 \\\\\nCE-MLP & 74.9 \\\\\nLR & 75.0 \\\\\nDeepSeek V3.2 (zero-shot)\\footnotemark & 40.0 \\\\\nppl-regression (single feature) & 25.1 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\footnotetext{Evaluated on 200 samples (approximately 50 per class) from the Pythia-1.4B nucleus cell only, due to API cost; 95\\% CI: $\\pm$6.8pp. All other methods use the full 20{,}000-sample set.}\n\\end{table}\n\nThat three structurally different classifiers achieve identical performance confirms that the discriminative signal resides in the 19 features, not the classifier architecture; a zero-shot LLM baseline (DeepSeek V3.2) reaches only 40.0\\%.\nFigure~\\ref{fig:confusion_matrix} shows d0 is near-perfectly identified in both settings; errors concentrate at the d2/d3 boundary in-distribution and spread across d1--d3 under cross-model transfer.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_confusion_matrix.pdf}\n\\caption{Confusion matrices for the best in-distribution setting (GPT-2 XL, temp $\\tau{=}0.9$, 85.4\\%) and the best cross-model leave-one-out transfer (OLMo holdout, 69.2\\%). d0 recall exceeds 99\\% in both; errors spread across the d1--d3 range under transfer.}\n\\label{fig:confusion_matrix}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Model Transfer}\n\\label{sec:gen:crossmodel}\n\nWe perform leave-one-model-family-out evaluation: train on two families (nucleus only), test on the held-out third (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_model}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cross-model transfer (leave-one-out, nucleus only). Train on 2 model families, test on held-out 3rd. Chance = 25\\%.}\n\\label{tab:cross_model}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\nHoldout & Acc & d0 & d1 & d2 & d3 \\\\\n & (\\%) & Rec. & Rec. & Rec. & Rec. \\\\\n\\midrule\nPythia & 62.4 & 100.0 & 49.3 & 26.8 & 73.6 \\\\\nOLMo & \\textbf{69.2} & 99.3 & 75.5 & 43.3 & 58.8 \\\\\nGPT-2 XL & 67.6 & 98.5 & 74.7 & 52.6 & 44.5 \\\\\n\\midrule\nMean & 66.4 & 99.3 & 66.5 & 40.9 & 59.0 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nMean transfer accuracy reaches 66.4\\% (2.7$\\times$ chance), though d0 recall ($>$98\\%) inflates the headline; d2 recall drops to 26.8\\% (Pythia holdout), making it the most confusable class.\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Strategy Transfer}\n\\label{sec:gen:crossstrategy}\n\nWe train on two decoding strategies and test on the held-out third (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_strategy}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cross-strategy transfer (leave-one-out). Train on 2 strategies, test on held-out 3rd. Chance = 25\\%.}\n\\label{tab:cross_strategy}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\nHoldout & Pythia & OLMo & GPT-2 XL \\\\\n & Acc (\\%) & Acc (\\%) & Acc (\\%) \\\\\n\\midrule\nnucleus & \\textbf{69.4} & \\textbf{65.4} & \\textbf{59.9} \\\\\ntemp ($\\tau{=}0.9$) & 62.8 & 58.2 & 56.1 \\\\\ntop-$k$ ($k{=}50$) & 51.5 & 54.9 & 53.6 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nMean cross-strategy accuracy (59.1\\%) falls 7 points below cross-model transfer (66.4\\%), indicating that strategy-induced distribution shifts pose a greater generalization challenge than architectural differences.\nAs with cross-model transfer, d0 recall remains high ($\\geq$89.5\\%) across all holdout settings, inflating the headline accuracy; d2 recall ranges from 3.7\\% to 19\\%, confirming mid-chain depths as the primary confusion locus across both transfer axes.\n\n\\paragraph{Temperature-induced boundary inversion.}\nTraining on nucleus + top-$k$ and testing on temperature yields d1v2 AUC of 0.129 for GPT-2~XL and 0.253 for OLMo---both below 0.5, indicating that the d1/d2 decision boundary \\emph{inverts} (Pythia retains a degraded 0.576), consistent with the accelerated convergence in \\S\\ref{sec:atlas:strategy}.\nZ-score normalization \\cite{sugiyama2007_importance_weighting} improves logistic regression cross-strategy accuracy (25\\%$\\to$61\\%) but does not recover the inversion---all three models worsen (GPT-2~XL 0.50$\\to$0.24, OLMo 0.50$\\to$0.19, Pythia 0.63$\\to$0.52)---confirming a genuine geometry-level shift rather than a correctable scale difference.\n\n\\subsection{End-to-End Learning}\n\\label{sec:gen:e2e}\n\nWe finetune DeBERTa-v3-base \\cite{he2021_deberta_v3} and RoBERTa-base \\cite{liu2019_roberta} end-to-end (lr$\\,{=}\\,2{\\times}10^{-5}$, 3 epochs, 5-fold CV) on Pythia-1.4B nucleus data.\nBoth converge to ${\\sim}$59\\% (RoBERTa 59.5$\\pm$3.2\\%, DeBERTa 58.3$\\pm$4.7\\%), 19pp below feature-based RF (78.2\\%); the 1.2pp inter-backbone gap rules out architecture selection bias.\nBoth easily separate d0 (F1$>$0.95) but fail at adjacent-depth discrimination (d1/d2 F1$<$0.42).\nProbing reveals a negative correlation between PLM attention and RF permutation importance for both backbones (DeBERTa $\\rho{=}{-}0.484$, RoBERTa $\\rho{=}{-}0.456$; zero top-5 overlap; Appendix~\\ref{app:feature_overlap}), confirming that PLMs encode surface lexical cues while our features capture perplexity tail structure inaccessible to end-to-end learning.\n\nDCOL \\cite{cloc2025}, which adds a contrastive ordinal loss, reached only 64.5\\% in its best configuration (RoBERTa-large; Appendix~\\ref{app:dcol_iterations}), 13.7pp below RF.\nThat all end-to-end approaches---finetuning (58--60\\%), DCOL (64.5\\%)---substantially underperform feature-based methods (78.2\\%) confirms that depth estimation requires explicit distributional statistics rather than learned text representations.",

P8: Probing reveals a negative correlation between
P8: PLM attention and RF permutation importance for
P8: both backbones (DeBERTa ρ=−0.484, RoBERTa
P8: ρ=−0.456; zero top-5 overlap; Appendix A.4),
P8: confirming that PLMs encode surface lexical cues
P8: while our features capture perplexity tail structure
P8: inaccessible to end-to-end learning.
P8: DCOL (Pitawela et al., 2025), which adds
P8: a contrastive ordinal loss, reached only 64.5%
P8: in its best configuration (RoBERTa-large;
P8: Appendix A.3), 13.7pp below RF. That all
P8: end-to-end approaches—finetuning (58–60%),
P8: DCOL (64.5%)—substantially underperform
P8: feature-based methods (78.2%) confirms that depth
P8: estimation requires explicit distributional statistics
P8: rather than learned text representations.
P8: Discussion
P8: Depth-graduated data curation. Predicted
P8: depth can serve as a quality proxy for importance
P8: weighting (Xie et al., 2023; Wettig et al., 2024),
P8: retaining shallow-generation text while downweighting deeply recursive samples that binary
P8: filters (Drayson et al., 2025) would indiscriminately discard. However, the temperature-induced
P8: d1/d2 AUC inversion (§5.3) means deploying
P8: without knowing the decoding strategy risks sys-
P8: Temperature-induced boundary inversion.
P8: Training on nucleus + top-k and testing on temperature yields d1v2 AUC of 0.129 for GPT-2 XL and
P8: 0.253 for OLMo—both below 0.5, indicating that
P8: the d1/d2 decision boundary inverts (Pythia retains
P8: a degraded 0.576), consistent with the accelerated
P8: convergence in §4.5. Z-score normalization

  "old_string": "We finetune DeBERTa-v3-base \\cite{he2021_deberta_v3} and RoBERTa-base \\cite{liu2019_roberta} end-to-end (lr$\\,{=}\\,2{\\times}10^{-5}$, 3 epochs, 5-fold CV) on Pythia-1.4B nucleus data.\nBoth converge to ${\\sim}$59\\% (RoBERTa 59.5$\\pm$3.2\\%, DeBERTa 58.3$\\pm$4.7\\%), 19pp below feature-based RF (78.2\\%); the 1.2pp inter-backbone gap rules out architecture selection bias.\nBoth easily separate d0 (F1$>$0.95) but fail at adjacent-depth discrimination (d1/d2 F1$<$0.42).\nProbing reveals a negative correlation between PLM attention and RF permutation importance for both backbones (DeBERTa $\\rho{=}{-}0.484$, RoBERTa $\\rho{=}{-}0.456$; zero top-5 overlap; Appendix~\\ref{app:feature_overlap}), confirming that PLMs encode surface lexical cues while our features capture perplexity tail structure inaccessible to end-to-end learning.\n\nDCOL \\cite{cloc2025}, which adds a contrastive ordinal loss, reached only 64.5\\% in its best configuration (RoBERTa-large; Appendix~\\ref{app:dcol_iterations}), 13.7pp below RF.\nThat all end-to-end approaches---finetuning (58--60\\%), DCOL (64.5\\%)---substantially underperform feature-based methods (78.2\\%) confirms that depth estimation requires explicit distributional statistics rather than learned text representations.",

    "newString": "We finetune DeBERTa-v3-base \\cite{he2021_deberta_v3} and RoBERTa-base \\cite{liu2019_roberta} end-to-end (lr$\\,{=}\\,2{\\times}10^{-5}$, 3 epochs, 5-fold CV) on Pythia-1.4B nucleus data.\nBoth converge to ${\\sim}$59\\% (RoBERTa 59.5$\\pm$3.2\\%, DeBERTa 58.3$\\pm$4.7\\%), 19pp below RF (78.2\\%); the 1.2pp gap rules out architecture bias.\nBoth separate d0 easily (F1$>$0.95) but fail at d1/d2 (F1$<$0.42).\nPLM attention and RF importance are negatively correlated (DeBERTa $\\rho{=}{-}0.484$, RoBERTa $\\rho{=}{-}0.456$; Jaccard$\\,{=}\\,0$; Appendix~\\ref{app:feature_overlap}): PLMs capture lexical cues while our features capture perplexity tail structure.\nDCOL \\cite{cloc2025} reached only 64.5\\% (RoBERTa-large; Appendix~\\ref{app:dcol_iterations}).\nAll end-to-end approaches (58--65\\%) substantially underperform feature-based methods (78.2\\%), confirming that depth estimation requires explicit distributional statistics.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Generalization and Robustness}\n\\label{sec:generalization}\n\nWe test whether the atlas results (69.6--85.4\\%) reflect genuine statistical regularities or training artifacts, evaluating four axes: classifier choice (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:baselines}), cross-model transfer (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:crossmodel}), cross-strategy transfer (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:crossstrategy}), and end-to-end learning (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:e2e}).\n\n\\subsection{Baseline Comparison}\n\\label{sec:gen:baselines}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:baselines} compares classification methods on the Pythia-1.4B nucleus setting.\nThree feature-based methods---Random Forest (RF), CORAL ordinal regression \\cite{coral2020}, and DivEye \\cite{diveye2026}---all operate on the same 19 statistical features and achieve statistically indistinguishable accuracy (78.1--78.2\\%; pairwise bootstrap $p>0.05$ after Bonferroni correction, 95\\% CI within $\\pm$1.1pp).\nThese three significantly outperform CE-MLP ($p<0.001$, $+$3.8pp), which in turn trails logistic regression by $\\sim$0.1pp.\nSingle-feature regression on mean perplexity performs at chance (25.1\\%), confirming that depth estimation requires the multi-dimensional feature representation defined in \\S\\ref{sec:method}.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Comparison of classification methods on Pythia-1.4B nucleus. RF, CORAL, and DivEye use the same 19 statistical features and are statistically indistinguishable (bootstrap $p>0.05$, Bonferroni-corrected). Chance = 25\\%. 5-fold CV std: 0.2--0.9pp across all methods.}\n\\label{tab:baselines}\n\\begin{tabular}{lc}\n\\toprule\nMethod & 4-class Acc (\\%) \\\\\n\\midrule\nRF (19 features) & 78.2 \\\\\nCORAL & 78.1 \\\\\nDivEye & 78.2 \\\\\nCE-MLP & 74.9 \\\\\nLR & 75.0 \\\\\nDeepSeek V3.2 (zero-shot)\\footnotemark & 40.0 \\\\\nppl-regression (single feature) & 25.1 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\footnotetext{Evaluated on 200 samples (approximately 50 per class) from the Pythia-1.4B nucleus cell only, due to API cost; 95\\% CI: $\\pm$6.8pp. All other methods use the full 20{,}000-sample set.}\n\\end{table}\n\nThat three structurally different classifiers achieve identical performance confirms that the discriminative signal resides in the 19 features, not the classifier architecture; a zero-shot LLM baseline (DeepSeek V3.2) reaches only 40.0\\%.\nFigure~\\ref{fig:confusion_matrix} shows d0 is near-perfectly identified in both settings; errors concentrate at the d2/d3 boundary in-distribution and spread across d1--d3 under cross-model transfer.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_confusion_matrix.pdf}\n\\caption{Confusion matrices for the best in-distribution setting (GPT-2 XL, temp $\\tau{=}0.9$, 85.4\\%) and the best cross-model leave-one-out transfer (OLMo holdout, 69.2\\%). d0 recall exceeds 99\\% in both; errors spread across the d1--d3 range under transfer.}\n\\label{fig:confusion_matrix}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Model Transfer}\n\\label{sec:gen:crossmodel}\n\nWe perform leave-one-model-family-out evaluation: train on two families (nucleus only), test on the held-out third (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_model}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cross-model transfer (leave-one-out, nucleus only). Train on 2 model families, test on held-out 3rd. Chance = 25\\%.}\n\\label{tab:cross_model}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\nHoldout & Acc & d0 & d1 & d2 & d3 \\\\\n & (\\%) & Rec. & Rec. & Rec. & Rec. \\\\\n\\midrule\nPythia & 62.4 & 100.0 & 49.3 & 26.8 & 73.6 \\\\\nOLMo & \\textbf{69.2} & 99.3 & 75.5 & 43.3 & 58.8 \\\\\nGPT-2 XL & 67.6 & 98.5 & 74.7 & 52.6 & 44.5 \\\\\n\\midrule\nMean & 66.4 & 99.3 & 66.5 & 40.9 & 59.0 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nMean transfer accuracy reaches 66.4\\% (2.7$\\times$ chance), though d0 recall ($>$98\\%) inflates the headline; d2 recall drops to 26.8\\% (Pythia holdout), making it the most confusable class.\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Strategy Transfer}\n\\label{sec:gen:crossstrategy}\n\nWe train on two decoding strategies and test on the held-out third (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_strategy}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cross-strategy transfer (leave-one-out). Train on 2 strategies, test on held-out 3rd. Chance = 25\\%.}\n\\label{tab:cross_strategy}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\nHoldout & Pythia & OLMo & GPT-2 XL \\\\\n & Acc (\\%) & Acc (\\%) & Acc (\\%) \\\\\n\\midrule\nnucleus & \\textbf{69.4} & \\textbf{65.4} & \\textbf{59.9} \\\\\ntemp ($\\tau{=}0.9$) & 62.8 & 58.2 & 56.1 \\\\\ntop-$k$ ($k{=}50$) & 51.5 & 54.9 & 53.6 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nMean cross-strategy accuracy (59.1\\%) falls 7pp below cross-model transfer (66.4\\%), indicating that strategy-induced shifts pose a greater challenge than architectural differences.\nAs with cross-model transfer, d0 recall remains high ($\\geq$89.5\\%), inflating headline accuracy; d2 recall ranges from 3.7\\% to 19\\%, confirming mid-chain depths as the primary confusion locus.\n\n\\paragraph{Temperature-induced boundary inversion.}\nTraining on nucleus + top-$k$ and testing on temperature yields d1v2 AUC of 0.129 for GPT-2~XL and 0.253 for OLMo---both below 0.5, indicating that the d1/d2 decision boundary \\emph{inverts} (Pythia retains a degraded 0.576), consistent with the accelerated convergence in \\S\\ref{sec:atlas:strategy}.\nZ-score normalization \\cite{sugiyama2007_importance_weighting} improves logistic regression cross-strategy accuracy ($25{\\%}{\\to}61{\\%}$) but does not recover the inversion---all three models' d1v2 AUC worsens---confirming a geometry-level shift rather than a correctable scale difference.\n\n\\subsection{End-to-End Learning}\n\\label{sec:gen:e2e}\n\nWe finetune DeBERTa-v3-base \\cite{he2021_deberta_v3} and RoBERTa-base \\cite{liu2019_roberta} end-to-end (lr$\\,{=}\\,2{\\times}10^{-5}$, 3 epochs, 5-fold CV) on Pythia-1.4B nucleus data.\nBoth converge to ${\\sim}$59\\% (RoBERTa 59.5$\\pm$3.2\\%, DeBERTa 58.3$\\pm$4.7\\%), 19pp below feature-based RF (78.2\\%); 1	\section{Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:conclusion}
3	
4	We introduced recursive generation depth estimation and showed that 19 lightweight statistical features---with p90 perplexity and surprisal variance as universal markers---achieve 70--85\% four-class accuracy (chance = 25\%) with $\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}{=}4$.
5	Cross-model transfer reaches 66.4\% and cross-strategy 59.1\%, though the temperature-induced d1/d2 inversion and PLMs' ${\sim}$59\% ceiling underscore remaining challenges; future work should address wild-data transfer, larger scales, and strategy-conditioned classifiers.
6	Code and data will be released upon acceptance.
7

    "originalFile": "\\section{Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nWe introduced recursive generation depth estimation and showed that 19 lightweight statistical features---with p90 perplexity and surprisal variance as universal markers---achieve 70--85\\% four-class accuracy (chance = 25\\%) with $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}{=}4$.\nCross-model transfer reaches 66.4\\% and cross-strategy 59.1\\%, though the temperature-induced d1/d2 inversion and PLMs' ${\\sim}$59\\% ceiling underscore remaining challenges; future work should address wild-data transfer, larger scales, and strategy-conditioned classifiers.\nCode and data will be released upon acceptance.\n\n\\section*{Limitations}\n\\label{sec:limitations}\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-strategy deployment constraint.}\nDeploying depth estimators across decoding strategies requires knowing the target strategy type a priori.\nWithout this knowledge, d1/d2 predictions may systematically invert: cross-strategy holdout d1v2 AUC drops to 0.129 (GPT-2~XL) and 0.253 (OLMo) when temperature-sampled text is classified by a model trained on nucleus and top-$k$ (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:crossstrategy}).\nStrategy-conditioned classifiers or automatic strategy identification are needed before deployment.\n\n\\paragraph{d0 inflation of headline metrics.}\nDepth~0 (human-written text) is near-perfectly separable (recall $>$98\\% across all settings), inflating headline accuracy.\nCross-model transfer accuracy of 66.4\\% is substantially driven by d0; d2 recall drops to 26.8\\% in the worst case (Pythia holdout).\n\n\\paragraph{Controlled vs.\\ ecological setting.}\nAll experiments use Wikipedia seed text with controlled recursive generation (LoRA fine-tuning, 1--1.5B parameter models).\nTransfer to mixed-domain corpora, instruction-tuned models, and larger scales (7B+) remains open.\nWild-data calibration is probed only indirectly; ecological transfer is future work.\n\n\\paragraph{EDD monotonicity assumption.}\nOur EDD definition (Eq.~\\ref{eq:edd}) requires all pairwise AUCs up to depth $d^*$ to exceed $T$, which is well-behaved when AUC decays monotonically---as observed in all our configurations.\nHowever, \\citet{bakshi2026_collapse_improvement} show that early recursion can temporarily \\emph{improve} model quality, which could produce non-monotonic AUC profiles (e.g., a dip at d1/d2 followed by recovery at d2/d3).\nIn such cases EDD would conservatively report a lower value than the deepest discriminable pair, potentially underestimating the true discriminable range; extending EDD to handle non-contiguous discriminable regions is future work.\nThe choice of threshold $T$ is also application-dependent: at $T{=}0.80$, several cells lose one depth level relative to $T{=}0.75$.\n\n\\paragraph{Reference model dependency.}\nSurprisal-based features are computed relative to GPT-2 base (124M), introducing partial overlap when GPT-2~XL is held out, though GPT-2~XL holdout accuracy (67.6\\%) does not exceed the unrelated OLMo (69.2\\%).\nThe choice of reference model affects feature values; a different reference model may shift absolute feature magnitudes and potentially alter discriminability at depth boundaries.\n\n\\paragraph{Additional caveats.}\nDetection features degrade under domain shift \\cite{pudasaini2026_detection_fails}; our depth features are likely susceptible.\n\\citet{falahati2026_curated} show that curated synthetic data need not trigger collapse, attenuating the fingerprints we exploit.",
